    - The server sends a protocol ping to each socket 25 s after its last one, looking every 5 s. It terminates a socket after 55 s with nothing heard: no pong and no frame. A socket that hasn't said `hello` within 10 s closes with 1008 `hello_timeout`.
    - The client sends `{ t: 'ping' }` every 25 s, and whenever it becomes visible or comes online. It treats 10 s without any frame after its ping as a dead socket.
    - Every Valkey subscriber connection sends `PING` every 5 s, so a dead one is found within about 7 s (its 2 s socket timeout).
    - The heartbeat isn't a revocation path.
    - *Why:* browsers can neither send nor see protocol pings. With 25 s of traffic each way, an ALB with the default 60 s idle timeout and most mobile NATs stay open.
    - **Readiness (OD-22).** There are two endpoints:
      - **The full `/ready`**, for deploy gating and monitoring. Its `gateway` check fails until realtime listens on `revoked` and `access`, while either subscription is down, while the stream reader is stuck (it has orgs and hasn't read for 7 s), while the last two periodic checks that had sockets to check have both failed (the point after which `closeUnconfirmed` starts closing with 1013), and while the process stops. It no longer checks object storage, which realtime never uses.
      - **`/ready/local`**, for the load balancer's health check. It fails only for reasons local to the process: before its first subscription to both channels, and while it stops.

      A Valkey, Postgres or S3 outage then fails every task's `/ready` but replaces no task, and sockets ride it out. With Valkey down, revoked sessions' sockets close at the re-checks instead; with Postgres down, a socket whose session or topics would go unconfirmed past 60 s closes with 1013.
    - **Drain.** At SIGTERM the process refuses upgrades, then closes every socket with 1001 within 1 s, and clients reconnect within the spread. On ECS the target is deregistered before SIGTERM, so the load balancer usually closes the sockets first and clients see 1006. The 1001 is the fallback, and both take the same spread.

13. **The latency metric (OD-20).** `apps/server/src/revocation/latency.ts`.
    - **When.** Each time the gateway applies a revocation (closes sockets, or drops granted topics), it logs `revocation_applied` with:
      - `handler` (`sockets` or `topics`);
      - `path` (`push`, `durable` or `recheck`);
      - `cause`, `org` and `count`;
      - `latencyMs`, measured from the change's own time;
      - `startSource`: `publisher`, `outbox`, `database`, or `unknown` (then `latencyMs` is null, and the summary counts it as `unknownStart`).

      A revocation message's session closes are one line per org. A check's closes and drops (targeted ones included, so a people signal's) are one line per person, from the earliest known start among them.
    - **Labels.** A check's session closes carry cause `ended`, whatever brought the check: an expiry or another session's end is no keys change. Its drops carry the cause of the trigger that asked for it (`keys_changed` for a periodic check).
      - A people signal's re-check takes the signal's path, since the signal is what brought in promptly what the change did (a deactivation's session ends included).
      - The re-check a session message queues for the person's other sessions is labelled `recheck`, since what it finds is by construction what the message didn't name. When it coalesces with a people signal for the same person, the signal's path wins.
      - So `closedRevoked` counts the push's own closes and those a re-check of the people it named finds, never an expiry that happened to sit next to a push.
    - **Start times.** Access is lost when the revoking transaction commits (or when an expiry passes), and each start names where it came from (`startSource`):
      - `publisher`: the push uses the api's `Date.now()` just after its commit.
      - `outbox`: the durable path's sessions message uses the `at` of the `signin.ended` event the revoking transaction appended (item 4). That is Postgres's `clock_timestamp()` when it was appended, after the writes that ended the sessions and every lock they waited for, so just before the commit. Neither start counts how long the revoking transaction ran, so the push's and the durable path's latencies can be compared.
      - `database`: a re-check uses Postgres's base rows for what ended:
        - a session's `revoked_at`, its device's `signed_out_at`, its person's `deactivated_at`, `expires_at` or the idle limit;
        - for a lost topic, the earliest time the base rows give for the change that took its token. A team topic's is the earlier of the person's deactivation and, when no membership of the team is active, the last removal. A person or org topic's is the deactivation.

        Realtime reads no outbox (item 14), so a re-check has no commit-side time. `revoked_at`, `signed_out_at`, `deactivated_at` and `removed_at` are each the revoking transaction's `now()`, its start, which comes before its commit by however long it ran: usually milliseconds, each lock wait at most 2 s (`lock_timeout`), the whole at most 30 s (`transaction_timeout`). A `database` latency can therefore read high by that much, never low: it may report a Rule 6 breach that didn't happen, never hide one. The stamps stay `now()`, because the deactivation, device sign-out and PIN reset find the rows they ended by `revoked_at = now()` and `signed_out_at = now()`. An expiry's and the idle limit's times are exact.
      - A people signal is measured from the base rows the re-check reads (`database`), whichever path brought it.
    - **Clamping.** The end time is realtime's `Date.now()` when the close or `dropped` frame is sent. Values are clamped to 1 ms–1 h. A latency below −1 s is counted (`skewed`), with its `skewMs`.
    - **Summaries and gauges.** Every 30 s the gateway logs:
      - `revocation_latency` per (handler, path, startSource), so no summary mixes starts from different points, with `count`, `p50`, `p95`, `p99`, `max`, `unknownStart`, `skewed` and `windowMs`. `max` is exact, and the percentiles (from `node:perf_hooks` `createHistogram`, to 3 significant figures) never exceed it;
      - `gateway_gauges`, with `oldestUnconfirmedMs`, socket, topic and org counts, the reader's lag, the spread, lookups running and waiting, decisions waiting, the recently-revoked set's size, refusals and closes by cause, `reset` frames sent, topics dropped by path, shed frames, topics answered `retry`, and `longestLatencyMs`, the longest revocation latency since the last gauges line (read before the summary starts a new window; null when none had a known start). It is written every 30 s while there are sockets, and once a minute otherwise.
    - **Levels.** Info up to 30 s, warn above 30 s, and error above 60 s (a Rule 6 breach). The gauges warn when `oldestUnconfirmedMs` is above 40 s, and are an error above 60 s.
    - No latency or gauges line carries a person's or session's id, a topic, a token, a phone number or content; the counts are per org.
    - C0.13 turns these into the "revocation latency" dashboard and its alarms.

14. **What realtime never does (OD-17, OD-41).**
    - Apart from C0.5's pre-org session lookup, every transaction it opens is read only. That lookup is a short read-write transaction whose `FOR SHARE` takes an xid for milliseconds, and it writes no row.
    - It writes nothing to Postgres. `scripts/guards/realtime-readonly.test.ts` enforces this, and its header names the lookup as the one exception.
    - It stamps no `last_seen_at`, keeps its `hearth_app` login (T18), reads no outbox, and imports no privileged module.
    - The per-socket lines (open, error, dead) are at debug, and the lines at info and above carry counts and org ids, never a person's or session's id, so its logs are no presence history (D34).

15. **An org with no system principal (OD-37).**
    - Its sockets close with 1013 `try again later`, never 4401, and the periodic check logs `ws_recheck_no_system_principal` at error level. The session may be live, and a 4401 would make C1.8's client wipe its cached data.
    - In a subscribe lane it is a lane failure: `retry`, and nothing closes.
    - Any other error fails the whole check, as before.

16. **Hosting (OD-31, OD-45; for C0.11 and C0.12).** These hold on C0a's minimal non-prod deploy too:
    - `/ws` is routed by path on the app's own host, never a subdomain. The `__Host-` cookie and the exact Origin check need it, and so does C0.8's CSP.
    - At least two realtime tasks, each sized for 25,000 sockets.
    - No stickiness.
    - An idle timeout of at least 60 s on the load balancer, and on CloudFront if it fronts `/ws`. The PRD's hour is fine.
    - A target-group deregistration delay of 10 s or less. ECS deregisters a task's target before SIGTERM, so clients usually see 1006 rather than the drain's 1001. The reconnect spread covers both (item 11).
    - Target-group health checks on `/ready/local`, never the full `/ready`. An outage of a shared dependency must not get every realtime task replaced at once.
    - A Valkey with `maxmemory-policy noeviction`, whose ACLs BUILD-PLAN C0.12 lists. Every service's user may run `CLIENT|SETNAME`, `CLIENT|SETINFO` and `INFO`, which ioredis 6 sends on every connect (the connection's name, its library's name and version, and its ready check), and `PING`, which Hearth sends itself (`/ready`'s check and the subscribers' keep-alive, item 12).
      - A refused `CLIENT` doesn't fail the connection: ioredis ignores it. A refused `CLIENT|SETNAME` leaves the connection unnamed; if only `CLIENT|SETINFO` is refused, it keeps its name and only its library's name and version (`lib-name`, `lib-ver` in `CLIENT LIST`) stay blank. Either way Valkey's ACL log gains a `NOPERM` entry for each refusal on every connect.
      - A refused `INFO` makes ioredis write a plain-text warning on every connect, outside the JSON logs.
      - `HELLO`, `AUTH` and `QUIT` need no grant: Valkey runs its no-auth commands whatever the ACL says.

17. **How the Done line is proved (OD-03, OD-38).**
    - Integration tests on the Compose stack prove it, with two realtime processes:
      - a deactivation closes every socket of the person within 60 s on each path: the push, the durable path alone (with the api's publish off), and the re-check alone;
      - a sign-out and a device sign-out close their sockets by the push and by the durable path alone, leaving the person's other sockets open;
      - a PIN reset and an admin's sign-out of someone's devices close their sockets by the push. Each appends `signin.ended` and ends the same session rows, so the durable path and the re-check treat them as they treat a sign-out.
      - The durable tests first wait for the consumer's checkpoint. They run on a realtime process whose periodic check can't fire during the test, so its per-path counters show that the durable path closed each socket or dropped each topic, not a check that read the change first.
    - Removal from a team drops the `team` topic within 60 s on each path, and keeps the socket and its other topics. The tests write the removal as the harness superuser, because C0.16 adds the first runtime producer.
      - The push path uses `publishAccess`, as C0.16's api will.
      - The durable path uses an appended `acl.changed` with C0.16's future reason.
      - The re-check runs alone.
    - Unit tests with fake timers prove the bound at production timings, with re-checks failing.
    - R6-10 is a c0 case for the decisions `can()` makes. The integration tests that prove the timing cite R6-10 in their names. R6-10b, a C1.1 todo, covers spaces.
    - **On a deployed stack** (C0a's minimal non-prod deploy, then prod for C0.19's exit), the proof is a drill. With sockets open on two realtime tasks, deactivate a test person and remove another from a team. Then read `revocation_applied` and `revocation_latency` for each path from C0.13's dashboard: every value is under 60 s. A person runs it, never a coding agent (`CLAUDE.md`: coding agents never connect to production), with synthetic test people, so no one's data is read.

18. **Recorded for later steps, not built.**
    - Restricted-room presence for non-participants (OD-32, owner, C2).
    - Chat queued offline after access was lost (OD-33, owner, C1.8).
    - Presence needs `presence_receipts` consent (OD-34, C1.3).
    - Public-space topics: read access or membership (OD-05, owner, C1.1).
    - The close's end reason (OD-42, owner, C1.8), keeping NFR-04 (item 10).
    - Hocuspocus may listen on `access` itself, or join as a registry handler (C4.3).
    - Finer resume, for chat (item 9; the owner's decision leaves it to C1.1): C1.1's per-conversation sequences in Postgres let a reconnecting client catch up over REST from the last sequence it holds, behind the same access checks, and C1.12 measures a reconnect storm's refetches. Nothing finer goes back into the gateway's frames: no frame carries a position read from the org's stream. C1.1's per-item kinds also check each entry at dispatch (`perItem`, item 7).
    - **Send-to-visible** (PRD §9.2: p95 under 300 ms, on the reference 4G link's 150 ms round trip). The fan-out hop (`XADD`, then `XREAD`, then the send) costs milliseconds in-region. But an ids-only `ev` costs the recipient one REST round trip, half the budget on that link. C1.1 fits that inside the budget, for example with one small fetch by id, or brings content in message frames (OD-14 (b)) to the owner and the DPO. C1.12 measures it.
    - **C5.6's slide snapshots.** The 4 KiB cap binds client frames only (ws `maxPayload`), so a server frame may be larger. But a snapshot is content, and content in a frame is OD-14 (b), the owner's and the DPO's decision, at C5.6.
    - **C1.3's presence** (PRD-58: changes reach others within 5 s) can't rest on the 25 s heartbeat, because a socket that dies silently is seen only at the dead limit. It uses explicit closes and visibility changes, and the heartbeat's numbers don't bind it.

## T20: sign-in by code, the defaults for the owner to confirm or change

The OD and AQ ids in this section are C0.8's owner decisions and the defaults C0.8 took, and the Q ids are the sign-in design's open points, all listed in the C0.8 PR (the open ones also in item 16). They are not T19's OD ids, which are C0.7's, nor the PRD's open questions (PRD §13). Outside T20 and T21 this file writes them as "C0.8 OD-01" and "C0.8 AQ-03". Every AQ id that code cites, and every Q id the sign-in code cites, is one this section records (`scripts/guards/design-ids.test.ts`).

The owner decided on 19 Oct 2026 (D46): sign-in by an SMS code through Twilio Verify, behind the SMS interface, with a local fake for development and CI, keeping the PIN and passkey code (OD-01); the PIN for holders of a PIN role and the code alone for everyone else (AQ-02); re-authentication by a fresh code for people without a PIN (AQ-01); and Twilio making and checking the code (AQ-03). In a third round the owner kept the names `PIN_ROLES` and `holdsPinRole` (item 5). Which roles are PIN roles (item 5) and the PIN-rule lifetime (item 7) are a Default the owner confirms; the rest are C0.8's choices. They are pinned by:
- `apps/server/src/auth/sign-in.integration.test.ts`, `codes.integration.test.ts` and the dormant `passkeys.integration.test.ts`;
- the unit tests beside them (`sign-in.test.ts`, `codes.test.ts`, `breaker.test.ts`, `policy.test.ts`, `attempts.test.ts`, `errors.test.ts`, `sessions.test.ts`, `sms/fake.test.ts`, `sms/hearth-codes.test.ts`, and `sms/twilio.test.ts` on the loopback stand-in, `testing/fake-twilio.ts`), with `config.test.ts`, `api.test.ts`, `access/world.test.ts` and `admin/people.test.ts`;
- `packages/access`'s `roles.test.ts` and `packages/shared`'s `auth.test.ts`;
- the race case in `apps/server/scripts/check-concurrency.ts`, and the guards `scripts/guards/sms-provider.test.ts`, `api-test-overrides.test.ts` and `design-ids.test.ts`.

The numbers live in `apps/server/src/auth/policy.ts` (`OTP`, `PIN_RULE_SESSION`, `LIMITS`, `PASSKEYS_ON`). C0.8 adds no migration.

1. **The provider interface** (`apps/server/src/auth/sms/provider.ts`; the name `SmsProvider` stays, AQ-15).
   - `start(to)` sends a code and answers the provider's opaque reference, which never holds the number or the code, and whether the provider handed back a verification this call didn't make (`foreign`, item 2).
   - `check(ref, code)` answers `approved`, `wrong` or `gone` (expired, cancelled, used or past the provider's own cap), and is never retried, since a check may have landed when its reply was lost.
   - `cancel(ref)` is best effort; an unknown or finished reference counts as done.
   - Each provider names the transfer-register entry it needs (`transfer`; none for the fake). A failure is an `SmsProviderError` with a kind, the provider's numeric code and the HTTP status, never the number, the code or the provider's message, which can quote the number. The provider is called only outside transactions.
   - The implementations: `sms/twilio.ts`, Twilio Verify (item 10); `sms/fake.ts`, Verify's semantics in memory, sending nothing (a second start for a number with a pending code hands back the same reference and code with one more send listed, so it reads as foreign, and a 6th send or check is refused); and `sms/hearth-codes.ts`, built now (AQ-16), where Hearth makes the code, a `TextSender` only delivers it, the reference is `h1.<nonce>.<MAC>`, and `check` recomputes the MAC in constant time. That is the shape the AWS End User Messaging fallback takes (T7), which takes over from Twilio only by a reviewed config change, never an automatic failover (AQ-14); no sender exists yet.
   - `createFakeVerify()`, the only path `main.ts` takes to the fake, refuses anywhere but this machine (T14 item 1) and prints `[local only, not a log line] Hearth code for <masked number>: <code>` to stderr, the same line for sign-in and re-authentication codes, which the e2e suite reads. The `FakeVerify` class prints nothing and is for tests.
2. **The challenge record** (`apps/server/src/auth/codes.ts`). One per number, `otp:{numberTag}`: a hash of the owner (the sign-in attempt's key, or `r:<session id>` for a re-authentication), the provider's reference, the tries counted and when it was made, on Valkey's clock. It never holds a code or a number. It lives 10 minutes, and checks are refused from 5 minutes after the request (PRD-53). Four Lua scripts compare and act, so a script that finds another owner's record, or none, changes nothing:
   - `OPEN` replaces any record for the number with the caller's, and answers the replaced reference, which the caller cancels before it starts its own code;
   - `ATTACH` sets the reference, only on the caller's record and only once;
   - `RESERVE` counts a try before the provider is asked, so parallel guesses stop at 5; past the 5 minutes it deletes the caller's own record and answers its reference, the only one a caller may cancel;
   - `SETTLE` applies the provider's verdict only while the record still holds the caller's owner and reference. A provider failure (no verdict) is settled first: it answers `failed` whatever the record, giving the try back only on the caller's own live record, so the caller gives the day's check back (item 3) even when a newer request replaced the record or another check used it meanwhile. Otherwise another owner's record, or another reference, answers `displaced` with the check's own try, and a record that is gone (another check killed or used it) answers `wrong`, or `dead` at the 5th, from the check's own try, whatever the provider answered. Neither changes anything.
   - A `gone` verdict marks the record gone instead of deleting it, and answers expired. The provider also answers gone to a check of a code it has just approved, so when two checks of the right code race on one attempt (two tabs, a double submit), the approval may settle after the gone one. A marked record answers every script as a deleted one would (`OPEN` no reference to cancel, `RESERVE` expired, `ATTACH` lost, `SETTLE` the check's own count, or `failed` with no try given back), except an approval of its own owner and reference, which still uses it up and signs in.

   A refusal depends only on the tries counted and the owner, never on whether a reference is attached, so a roster number (which has one) answers exactly as one off the roster (which never has one). So a stale attempt can't delete or cancel the newest owner's code, spend or refund one of its tries, or kill a signed-in person's pending re-authentication code.
   - **A displaced check (changed in A3's review, Q27).** A check that a newer request for the number overtook during the provider's round trip signs nobody in, even when the provider approved it. On sign-in it answers from its own try, `code_wrong {triesLeft}` or at the 5th `code_dead`, and the attempt's next check answers `code_expired` for every number alike. The design first answered it `code_expired`, which told a roster number apart: off the roster `RESERVE` fixes the reply at once, before any newer request can land. Re-authentication, signed-in traffic with nothing to hide, answers it `code_expired`.
   - **The foreign check.** Twilio reuses a pending verification for the same number on the same Service (same reference, same code). After every start the deliverer asks the record whether its owner still holds it. A request that has lost the record leaves the verification alone, so it never cancels a newer owner's. One that still holds it and got a foreign verification cancels it and starts once more; a second foreign reply is cancelled too and nothing is attached (logged as `provider_foreign_verification` at warn), so the attempt answers as after a lost text. If that first cancel fails, nothing more is started or attached, and the failure is handled as a failed send (`sms_send_failed`; re-authentication answers 503): the verification is most likely still pending, so a second start would only hand it back and text its code again. The next request tries the cancel again. This defends against a verification planted with a stolen key (item 10). Residual, kept on review (Qodo): a start whose first try is counted as a send that Twilio hadn't recorded when it answered the retry (a dropped connection, a timeout Twilio handles after the retry, or a 5xx that sent nothing) can read a pending verification this call didn't start, listing exactly one send, as its own and attach it. Its code was made by Twilio and texted to the same number (custom codes stay off, item 10), so a code first texted a few minutes earlier, within Twilio's validity, finishes the new attempt; every check is still bounded by this record's 5 minutes and 5 tries and by Twilio's own 5 checks. Reading every reply after an uncertain try as foreign would instead cancel and restart on each provider glitch: an extra paid text, the code already sent dead, and nothing attached after two glitches in a row. The owner kept it as it is on 21 Oct 2026.
3. **The floor and the breaker** (`api.otp`, built per `registerApi` from `OTP`, so each api process has its own).
   - A refused sign-in check (`code_wrong`, `code_dead`, `code_expired`), and one the provider failed (503 `unavailable`), answers no sooner than 2 s (`OTP.verifyFloorMs`, AQ-04) after it arrived, so a roster number's round trip to the provider can't be timed against the instant answer for a number off the roster. Successes aren't padded, nor is the open breaker's 503, which answers every number alike without asking the provider.
   - After 3 provider failures within 60 s, every check, for any number, answers 503 `unavailable` for 30 s without asking the provider (`OTP.breaker`, AQ-20). A failure gives the try back on the caller's own live record (item 2), and the day's check in every case, even when a newer request replaced the record or another check used it meanwhile (`otp-v24h`, or `reauth-check-p24h` for re-authentication; the per-IP and per-session windows still count the request), and a sign-in check the open breaker refuses counts in no window, since it is refused before them. So a person who presses again through a provider incident spends none of the day's checks (T14 item 5).
   - Every provider cancel on `/otp/verify` runs after the reply, so a roster number's check costs one round trip before it, whatever the try.
   - Tests may shorten the floor and set a breaker of their own through `ApiDeps.otpForTests`, which `scripts/guards/api-test-overrides.test.ts` keeps out of production code; integration tests on the real timing pin the 2 s.
4. **The `verified` step** (`apps/server/src/auth/attempts.ts`: `code_sent`, `verified`, `set_pin`, `step_up`). Once the provider approves a code the attempt is `verified`, its record is used up, and the finish follows in the same request. If the finish fails before it uses the attempt up (409 `try_again`, Valkey or Postgres down), a retry with any 6 digits finishes it with no second check, counted only in the per-IP window, until the attempt's 10 minutes end. The code-only finish uses the attempt up as its last statement before `COMMIT`, so only one request turns it into a session. A finish that finds the person has left or the number has moved answers as a wrong guess at that count and puts the attempt back at the code, whose record is gone, so its next check answers `code_expired`; on a `verified` retry it drops the attempt (401 `attempt_expired`).
   - Residual: using the attempt up is a Valkey delete that runs before Postgres's `COMMIT`. A `COMMIT` that then fails (a lost connection, or the 30 s `transaction_timeout`), or a delete whose reply was lost, leaves no attempt and nothing committed, so the person asks for a new code. It fails safe: no session is issued twice.
5. **The PIN rule** (`packages/access/src/model.ts` `PIN_ROLES`, `reauth.ts` `holdsPinRole`).
   - `PIN_ROLES` equals `OWNER_ONLY_ROLES`: Owner, Admin, AI Admin, Leadership, DPO and Compliance Officer. `roles.test.ts` pins the two lists equal, so splitting them is a reviewed change. It is a code constant, never a flag (`CLAUDE.md`, Database). Team Admin and Member sign in on the code alone.
   - Whether someone holds a PIN role is read in the transaction that acts on it (`holdsPinRoleNow`, `apps/server/src/access/world.ts`): after their person row is read `FOR SHARE`, as its own statement, with no lock on `role_assignments`. The person's row is where a sign-in and a grant meet: a grant that holds that row, as C0.16's will (item 14), makes the finish wait and then see the role (the race case in `check-concurrency.ts`).
   - The code-only finish reads the roles, makes the person's `users` row on their first sign-in (beside `signin.started`) and issues the session in one transaction. `/pin/set`, `/pin/verify` and the dormant `/passkey/verify` read the roles again where they issue the session, which is then issued under the PIN rule. A role removed between the code and the PIN step still finishes with the PIN (stricter), with a normal lifetime.
   - Three invariants hold the rule. I1 (C0.8): a sign-in issues a session only after reading the roles so, and to a PIN-role holder only through the PIN step. I2 (C0.8): each re-authentication route refuses a session whose method isn't its own, deciding in the transaction that stamps (item 6). I3 (C0.16): any write that gives someone a PIN role first takes their person row `FOR NO KEY UPDATE`, then ends their open sessions, then writes the role row, in one transaction (item 14).
   - The names: the design called them `PRIVILEGED_ROLES` and `isPrivileged`. In this repo "privileged" means Rule 9 access, and R9-02 refuses any `packages/access` export whose name says it, so they became `PIN_ROLES` and `holdsPinRole`, and the owner kept them (third round). The sign-in code says "PIN role" and "PIN rule" throughout (`PIN_RULE_SESSION`, `holdsPinRoleNow`, `issuedUnderPinRule`, `pin_rule` on `signin.started`, `pinRule` on `auth.sign_in`); every Rule 9 use of "privileged" stays.
6. **Re-authentication by method** (`reauthMethodOf`, `packages/access/src/reauth.ts`; `apps/server/src/auth/account.ts`). Decided by the session first:
   - a session issued under the PIN rule re-authenticates with the PIN (`pin`), whatever its holder's roles are now, so no SMS stamp ever lands on it; if its PIN has been cleared, only a new sign-in mends it (`sign_in`);
   - any other session uses a fresh SMS code to the person's own number (`code`), unless its holder holds a PIN role now, which a grant that didn't end their sessions would cause and C0.16 must never do: then only a new sign-in, through the PIN step, mends it (`sign_in`).

   The server reads the session's raw facts in the deciding transaction (`actingSession`: a stamp from the last 5 minutes, whether the person has a PIN, and whether the session was issued under the PIN rule), and `can()` still receives one boolean, `reauthenticated()`, false for `sign_in` even with a fresh stamp. "Issued under the PIN rule" is read as `expires_at - created_at` shorter than `SESSION.lifetime`: only `issueSession` writes `expires_at`, from the same `now()` as `created_at`, and `policy.test.ts` keeps `PIN_RULE_SESSION` shorter. It is defence in depth behind I1 and I3, not the primary control.
   - A stale admin request answers 403 `reauth_required {method}`. A re-authentication route that isn't the session's own answers 409 `reauth_method {method}` before anything is checked or sent, and writes nothing (it counts only in the route's windows); it is logged as `reauth_wrong_method` at warn, since the web app only calls the route the method names, and reaches no audit row (Q5). A `sign_in` method also logs `pin_rule_session_mismatch` at warn. Both lines hold the person's id, and the wrong-method line the route, never the method, a number or a code.
   - From C0.9 PR 2 every re-authentication route asks the acknowledgement gate first (`actingBy()`, with the use `'reauth'`; T22 item 28): a session whose person hasn't acknowledged the current notice answers 403 `notice_required` before the method guard, so it can't make the provider send a code. Before any version is published only the DPO passes, by PIN (T22 item 29).
   - `POST /api/auth/reauth/pin`: the per-session window, which lets requests through with Valkey down, then the PIN check and its lock, as at sign-in (T14 item 9).
   - `POST /api/auth/reauth/code/request`: the per-session window, failing closed (AQ-10); one request per session at a time, under a send lock (`reauth-send:<session id>`, `SET NX` for at most 15 s, `REAUTH.sendLockMs`, freed only by its holder), since every request from one session owns the record as `r:<session id>` and two at once would both pass the start's owner check, so a double tap answers 409 `try_again` before anything is opened or sent (a request slower than the lock no longer holds it); the method guard; the person's current number, read with no lock, since `phone_numbers` comes before devices and sessions in the lock order (none: 409 `no_number`, AQ-23); the transfer gate and the global SMS cap, each audited and answered 503; 20 code requests per person a rolling day that pass the transfer gate and the SMS cap, failing closed (AQ-09), so a request either holds back spends none of the person's day (from C0.9 PR 2, T22 item 27), and a full day answers 429 and gives the cap's count back; then the record, owned by `r:<session id>`, and the text, sent before the reply (signed-in traffic, nothing to hide), so a failed send answers 503.
   - `POST /api/auth/reauth/code/verify`: the per-session window and 20 code checks per person a rolling day, failing closed (AQ-09); the method guard before any provider check is spent; the breaker; no floor. A check the provider never answered (the breaker open, or the provider failing) gives the day's check back, and so does one refused before any check (from C0.9 PR 2: the acknowledgement gate's 403 `notice_required`, the method guard, or a session that ended). An approved code whose stamp fails before it commits (409 `try_again`, Postgres lost) is kept for 2 minutes (`REAUTH.approvedMs`) under `reauth-ok:<session id>`, holding the number's id, so a retry with any 6 digits stamps the session with no second check, as sign-in's `verified` step does (item 4); it counts only in the per-session window, reads the method and the number again, and uses the approval up as its last statement before `COMMIT`. A new code request from the session deletes the approval before it opens the record, so from then on only the new code stamps it. Residual, as item 4's: a `COMMIT` that fails after the approval is used up asks for a new code. An approved code stamps the session in a second transaction, which reads the method again (a grant committed since refuses, and the spent code stamps nothing) and the number, with no lock: `actingSession` has already taken the person, device and session `FOR SHARE`, and the person's row serialises this against a phone change (C0.16), which takes it `FOR NO KEY UPDATE` first and ends every session.
   - Re-authentication codes don't count against sign-in's per-number windows, so a working hour of re-authentication can't lock someone out of signing in. They share the number's one record, as Twilio holds one pending verification per number per Service: a re-authentication code replaces a pending sign-in code, and the reverse (T14 item 3's residual).
   - Outages: Valkey down stops SMS re-authentication but not the PIN's; Twilio down stops SMS re-authentication and every sign-in, but not a live PIN-rule session's PIN re-authentication. So PIN-role holders keep their admin powers through either.
   - C0.8's web app holds the contract only: `ReauthMethod` (`packages/shared/src/auth.ts`, its one definition), the `method` on both refusals, read by `apps/web/src/api/reauth.ts` (a method it doesn't know reads as `sign_in`), and the code and PIN fields (`ui/CodeField.tsx`, `ui/PinField.tsx`). C0.9's DPO pages are the first admin actions in the UI, and they re-authenticate inline with the PIN, then send the refused request once more (T22 item 15); C0.16's re-authentication sheet replaces that step.
7. **Sessions issued under the PIN rule** (`PIN_RULE_SESSION`, AQ-05/06): 168 hours (7 days), set at issue in `sessions.expires_at` as the 90-day lifetime is, so every lookup, the gateway's upgrade and its 15 s re-check enforce it with no role read, and the cookie's `Max-Age` follows. In hours, so daylight saving can't stretch it, and within 0006's 90-day cap, so no migration. It is fixed at issue: tuning it, or the role list, applies to new sessions only, and the session-first rule covers admin actions meanwhile. `signin.started` carries `pin_rule` and `auth.sign_in` `pinRule`. The idle limit stays 30 days for everyone: a shorter one for these sessions would need a role read in `session_by_token`, a migration (Q10).
8. **Passkeys dormant** (`PASSKEYS_ON = false`, `apps/server/src/auth/policy.ts`). A passkey stands in for a set PIN, and only PIN-role holders have PINs.
   - While off, the seven passkey routes aren't registered (an `/api` path with no route answers 404 with `no-store`), the step-up reply has no `passkey` key, and `GET /api/devices` lists no passkeys. The code, the `passkeys` table, its grants, coverage entries and events stay, and `@simplewebauthn/server` still loads at start, so it stays under `npm audit` and Renovate. The dormant re-authentication routes take only a session whose method is the PIN.
   - `policy.test.ts` pins the constant false; it is never a flag or an env var. Only tests switch passkeys on (`ApiDeps.passkeysForTests`): the dormant integration suite (`apps/server/src/auth/passkeys.integration.test.ts`), through the harness, and one `api.test.ts` body-limit case. The suite keeps the code working and requires every error and audit action that only it can reach (`testing/dormant.ts`). `scripts/guards/api-test-overrides.test.ts` keeps the override out of production code and `PASSKEYS_ON` assigned only in `policy.ts`. The web app has no WebAuthn ceremony (WS-12).
   - Turning passkeys back on is a reviewed PR: the constant and its test, D46 and T14 item 10, registration for people without a PIN role, and production's final host name.
9. **The transfer gate** (PDPA-10). Before a provider that names a transfer-register entry receives a number, sign-in and re-authentication ask whether that entry is cleared. C0.8 built the question on `ApiDeps.gate`, which defaulted to `C06_GATE` and cleared no provider. C0.9 replaced it with `Api.transfers` (`DB_TRANSFERS`, set in `registerApi`), which asks `transfer_cleared()` in Postgres, `FOR SHARE`, in the transaction that decides the send, and calls the provider only after that transaction commits, with the bound T22 item 8 states; `ApiDeps.gate` is gone. While a provider isn't cleared, nothing reaches it: the send is skipped, audited as `auth.code_requested {send: 'skipped_not_cleared'}` and logged at error; sign-in's reply stays the same as for any number, and a re-authentication request answers 503. So Twilio can be configured in non-prod and sends nothing until its entry is cleared: the owner's "PDPA-10 before any real code", in code. From C0.9 PR 2 both codes ask `transfer_send_state()` instead, which also says whether the published notice states the clearance, and a code goes to everyone only while it does; before any version, and while the published one doesn't state it, only a holder of `dpo` gets one (`codeMaySend()`, C0.9 PD-59). Everyone else's is skipped the same way, audited `skipped_no_notice` (logged at info, expected before v0) or `skipped_unstated` (logged at error as `sms_provider_unstated`) (T22 item 27). `PROVIDERS` (`packages/shared/src/purposes.ts`) gains `twilio`; `aws_sms` stays.
10. **Twilio's adapter** (`apps/server/src/auth/sms/twilio.ts`).
    - Plain `fetch`, no `twilio` package (AQ-21): its automatic retry repeats 429s Twilio says not to retry, and it would add 48 packages.
    - A Restricted API Key scoped to Verify, sent as HTTP Basic, and the Verify Service's SID (`TWILIO_API_KEY_SID`, `TWILIO_API_KEY_SECRET`, `TWILIO_VERIFY_SERVICE_SID`; from Secrets Manager in C0.12). The key holds create Verification, create VerificationCheck and update Verification only: never a Service update, which could turn custom codes on, nor any permission outside Verify.
    - Twilio receives the number, the channel and the Service's SID: no name, device IP or tags. Hearth logs Twilio's numeric code and HTTP status only, never its message, a body or the auth header, and Valkey holds Twilio's reference and counts, never a number or a code.
    - Every request: redirects refused, a timeout, a 64 KiB cap before parsing, and zod on the fields used. `start`: 4 s a try, and one retry, only on a network error, a 5xx or 20429. `check`: one try of 4 s, never retried; 404 or 60202 reads as `gone`, any 2xx is an answer, and only `approved` approves. `cancel`: 3 s, no retry; 404 counts as done.
    - No webhook, so no exemption from the Origin hook, and not on `/ready`.
    - Where it may run: config refuses `SMS_PROVIDER=twilio` where `DEPLOY_ENV` is `local` or `test` **or** `APP_ORIGIN` is a localhost origin, and `createTwilioVerify()`, which fixes the base URL to Twilio's, refuses the same independently (any `DEPLOY_ENV` but `nonprod` or `prod`), so two conditions guard it as they guard every machine-only setting. A localhost origin is any that `isLoopbackOrigin()` (`apps/server/src/config.ts`) knows: the name `localhost` or any name under it, with or without a trailing dot, 127.0.0.0/8, `::1`, and the unspecified `0.0.0.0` and `::`, each also IPv4-mapped. It allows the fake and the local `AUTH_SECRET` and refuses Twilio, so it must miss none. `scripts/guards/sms-provider.test.ts` names Twilio's host only in `twilio.ts`, calls the guarded factories only from `main.ts` (their own tests aside), and keeps the loopback stand-in in tests.
    - **Two preconditions code can't check.** Twilio reuses a pending verification for the same number, so if custom codes were on, anyone able to create verifications could plant one with a code they chose, and Hearth's own check would approve it. So the Service's `CustomCodeEnabled` stays false, and the key has no Service-update permission. The foreign check (item 2) replaces a planted verification with one Twilio makes, unless it is planted again in the moment between Hearth's cancel and its second start.
    - The Service's settings, made by a person from a runbook (AQ-27, one subaccount per environment): name Hearth, code length 6, the do-not-share warning on, lookup off, custom codes off. Twilio Support may set the validity to 5 minutes (AQ-07); Hearth enforces 5 regardless. Any Console user can change these settings, so Console access to each subaccount is limited to the people the runbook names (Q25).
11. **Twilio's go-live gates** (C0.19's checklist). A person checks them in non-prod after C0.9, before production sends anything, and again after any change to the Service:
    1. a verification approved by an update answers 404 to a later check, which Hearth reads as expired, so a stolen key's "approve" signs nobody in;
    2. a verification created with a custom code is refused (`CustomCodeEnabled` false);
    3. the key can't update the Service;
    4. a second start for the same number answers the same reference with two `send_code_attempts`, so the foreign check works.

    Confirmed in writing where Twilio says it (AQ-19, Q13): Verify's log retention and the countries it stores in, which from C0.9 are facts Twilio's transfer-register entry needs before it is first cleared, so they come before the bootstrap clears it (T22 item 3); and, with the gates, that a cancel and then a start gives a new code. Set up before C0.19 (AQ-17, Q14): Fraud Guard Standard, Geo Permissions narrowed to the team's countries, RCS opted out (the DPO's call), the domain-bound template, English, and the SSIR sender. Narrowing Geo Permissions refines the owner's 13 Oct 2026 decision that any country's number may sign in (T14 item 2), so it needs the owner's confirmation: a roster number from a country left out gets no sign-in code, with the same reply as everyone's. The runbook therefore adds a country before a number from it joins the roster.
12. **The enrolment window** (an accepted risk, Q24). A PIN-role holder's first PIN is set on the SMS code alone: after a grant to someone with no PIN, an Owner's reset, the bootstrap's first Owner and DPO, or a phone change (C0.16). Between that and the holder's own first sign-in, whoever holds the number (a SIM swap, a port-out) can sign in, set the PIN and get a PIN-rule session, and the holder is locked out (`pin_already_set`, then a PIN they don't know); they notice, but only afterwards. What limits it: procedure (the holder signs in at once, with the granting or resetting Owner present or on the call), C0.16's console ("PIN not set yet"), keeping a former holder's PIN on removal so a re-grant needs it (Q3), and C0.14's alert, which treats `sms_new_pin` as urgent but fires only once the session exists: it detects a takeover, it doesn't prevent one.
13. **Residuals.**
    - Members and Team Admins rest on one SMS: whoever receives their texts (a SIM swap, a port-out, interception, a linked device) can sign in as them on a new device, for up to 90 days. From C0.16 a Team Admin's team powers need only a fresh code, which is why leaving Team Admin out of the PIN roles is the owner's to confirm (Q2).
    - There is no phishing-resistant factor while passkeys are off: a relayed code and a phished PIN still work against a PIN-role holder.
    - Twilio joins the trust base. It makes and checks the code, and a stolen key can send texts at Hearth's cost (with any sender name), read Verify's logs (numbers), cancel pending codes and mark a verification approved. Hearth trusts only `approved` from its own check, and an approval by update answers 404 to a later check (item 11, gate 1).
    - The floor's slow-check leak, the outage's 503 before the breaker opens, and a sign-in request replacing a pending re-authentication code (T14 item 3).
    - A role removed between `/otp/verify` and the PIN step leaves a PIN hash nothing asks for until a re-grant.
    - A PIN-role power that needs no fresh stamp rests on I1 and I3 alone: today one, an Admin creating public channels or standalone rooms (of any visibility, ROOM-01) while the setting is `admins` (`packages/access/src/can.ts` `publicCreation`).
    - The success reply (`signed_in` or `code_verified`) shows whether someone holds a PIN role, but only to the holder of the right code; refusals are padded to the floor.
    - A PIN-role holder whose PIN is locked until a reset can't sign in until an Owner resets it, with no passkey to fall back on, and a sole Owner needs the bootstrap's break-glass path (the PIN reset's PR, before C0.15; C0.9 OD-28), so at least two Owners are recommended (Q6).
    - Cost: each sign-in without a live session and each SMS re-authentication is a paid verification. `sms-global` caps the code sends Hearth asks of the provider at 100 an hour, counting each code request once, so a start's one retry after a network error or a 5xx (when Twilio had sent already), and a foreign reply's cancel and second start (item 2), can each add a text it doesn't count.
14. **What C0.16 inherits.**
    1. I3 in the lock order. Every write that gives someone a PIN role (a grant; the approval of a DPO or Compliance Officer proposal, which inserts the role row; a roster row; the bootstrap) takes their person row `FOR NO KEY UPDATE` after the actor's rows, then ends all their open sessions (`role_changed`), then inserts the role row: people → sessions → role_assignments. It appends `signin.ended`, audits, and publishes after commit, as the PIN reset does. The caller ends the sessions, not a trigger, since only the caller can publish after commit. The database backs it: `role_assignments_check`, for a PIN role given to someone who holds none yet, re-takes their person row `FOR NO KEY UPDATE NOWAIT` (never waiting, so it can't deadlock against the order) and refuses while they have a live session. Its SQL list of the roles gets a CI check against `PIN_ROLES` in `check-catalogues.ts`, and C0.16 adds concurrency cases for both sides.
    2. A migration adds `role_changed` and `phone_changed` to the sessions' revoke reasons (0006's NOT VALID pattern), with item 1's trigger change; `signin.ended`'s reason follows.
    3. Removing someone's last PIN role keeps their PIN by default (Q3), so a re-grant needs it, and the session-first rule keeps their PIN-rule sessions on the PIN meanwhile. Clearing it is the minimisation choice, at the cost of an SMS-only enrolment on every re-grant.
    4. ACC-06's phone change ends every session (`phone_changed`) and clears the PIN only for a PIN-role holder, which is an SMS-only enrolment on the new number, so the admin checks the person first.
    5. The PIN reset is offered only for someone with a PIN, and its form says the person signs in straight away, with the Owner present or on the call.
    6. The console grants a PIN role only with a confirmation that the grantee is present or has been told to sign in at once, and shows "PIN not set yet" beside every PIN-role holder without one.
    7. An option to weigh: enrolling from the holder's existing session (its cookie, a fresh code and a new PIN issue a new PIN-rule session and revoke the old one), leaving SMS-only enrolment to people with no live session. It changes I3 and item 1's check.
    8. The re-authentication sheet for `reauth_required {method}`, which replaces the inline PIN step of the first admin actions in the UI, C0.9's DPO pages (T22 item 15).
    9. Optionally, a `sessions.reauth_method` column that moves "a PIN role needs the PIN" into `adminCan` (Q4; the default relies on I1 to I3 and the session-first rule).
15. **What C0.14 inherits.** The DPO alert: the notification consumer reads `signin.started` with `pin_rule: true`, never the roles at alert time (every sign-in makes a new device row, so every such sign-in is on a new device). It treats `method: 'sms_new_pin'` (a first PIN set on the code alone, item 12) as urgent, and C0.14 decides who is alerted when the DPO signs in (default: the DPO and every Owner but the signer, Q9).
16. **Open points for the owner** (listed in the C0.8 PR; the sign-in design's numbering, not the PRD's):
    - Q1, the PIN-rule lifetime: 168 hours; stricter is 24 or 12, at a text, a PIN and (from C0.14) a DPO alert per holder per working day.
    - Q2, which roles are PIN roles: the six Owner-only roles, Team Admin left out.
    - Q3, keep a former holder's PIN on removal (with the DPO): keep.
    - Q24, the enrolment window: accepted, kept short by procedure (item 12).
    - Q25, who has Console access to Twilio's subaccounts.
    - Q6, at least two Owners: yes.
    - Q7, the sign-in and re-authentication copy.
    - Q8, PINs in password managers: allowed (WCAG 3.3.8), though a synced manager weakens the PIN's separation from the phone.
    - Q11, D46's wording and the PRD edits.
    - Q13, who checks the go-live gates (item 11).
    - Q14, Twilio's set-up before C0.19, including Geo Permissions narrowed to the team's countries, which refines the 13 Oct 2026 "any country" decision (item 11).
    - Q4, Q5 (a wrong-method re-authentication is logged, not audited), Q9 and Q10, which proceed on their defaults (items 14, 6, 15 and 7) unless the owner asks otherwise.
    - Q27, a displaced check answered from its own try (item 2), changed in review and listed for the owner to see.

    Decided, not open: the names `PIN_ROLES` and `holdsPinRole` (item 5).

## T21: the web shell, defaults for the owner to confirm or change

The OD ids in this section are C0.8's owner decisions and the defaults C0.8 took, listed in the C0.8 PR (OD-01 and the AQ ids are sign-in's, T20); the D-01 to D-09 ids are the shell design's own choices, recorded here. They are not T19's OD ids, which are C0.7's, nor the PRD's decisions (D1 to D46). The shell design's working ids (its OD-19 onwards and its R-, K- and C- lists) aren't recorded, and nothing cites them: code that needs one cites the item here that holds the choice.

The owner decided the five tabs (OD-02), the hidden Search bar (OD-04) and the hosting (OD-11) on 19 Oct 2026; `CLAUDE.md` decides the stack. On 21 Oct 2026 the owner decided that Hearth uses the-newnormal/finance's visual guidelines, landing in a PR after C0.8's, with Hearth's own flame mark recoloured in finance's palette (item 6). The same day the owner asked for WebKit's CSP reports to reach `POST /api/csp-report`, which the Origin hook refused; item 9's one exception to the hook is how. The rest are C0.8's choices, and the re-theme PR's choices where the owner's decision left one open, each a Default the owner confirms or changes in the PR that made it. They are pinned by:
- the unit tests beside the code in `apps/web/src`: kit and screen markup rendered with `react-dom/server` in Node, and the logic as pure functions (there is no DOM environment);
- the guards `scripts/guards/web-source.test.ts`, `csp.test.ts`, `csp-report.test.ts`, `e2e-setup.test.ts` and `web-dist.test.ts`;
- `packages/shared`'s `web.test.ts` and `web-headers.test.ts`, the server's `web/routes.test.ts`, `web/flags.test.ts`, `web/csp-report.test.ts`, `web/routes.integration.test.ts`, `origin.test.ts` and `app.test.ts`, and `packages/access`'s role matrix;
- the Playwright suite in `apps/web/e2e`.

1. **Stack.** Vite 8, React 19.3, Tailwind 4.3, TanStack Router 1.170 (routes in code, no generated file) and TanStack Query 5.104, zod 4, lucide icons, and finance's two families self-hosted through Fontsource: Inter (`@fontsource-variable/inter`, the upright weight axis) for text and Newsreader (`@fontsource/newsreader`, weight 600 upright only) for headings (item 6). No headless UI library, no devtools, no jsdom or Testing Library. `apps/web` compiles against ES2023 (D-09), so tsc refuses the library APIs and syntax Safari on iOS 16.4, the pilot's floor, lacks (`Promise.withResolvers`, `Object.groupBy`, the regex `v` flag); the DOM types aren't narrowed, so WS-08 refuses the Popover API. It has two tsconfigs, `tsconfig.json` for the browser code in `src/` and `tsconfig.node.json` for its Node side (the e2e suite and the configs), and WS-09 keeps every file in exactly one. `src/` imports no Node module (Biome's `noNodejsModules`) and reads no `process.env` (WS-08).
2. **A session is a document** (D-01, D-02; `apps/web/src/session/document.ts`, `session/runtime.ts`).
   - Every change of person ends in a full page load: sign-in, sign-out, a 401 `signed_out`, the gateway's 4401, a session answer (or, from C0.9 PR 2, a notice answer) naming someone else, or another tab's sign-in or sign-out (a `BroadcastChannel` message carrying no id and no data). A page restored from the back-forward cache reloads. So the QueryClient, the gateway client, the router and every module's state live exactly as long as one person's document.
   - Acknowledging the notice (C0.9 PR 2, T22 item 31) changes no person, so nothing reloads: the runtime's notice store starts or resumes the gateway and refetches every query, and the same channel's `{t:'notice'}`, which carries no id and no data, tells the browser's other tabs to check; older tabs ignore it. The path a tab was sent from to `/notice` stays in the runtime's memory, never in storage.
   - The runtime's `end()` runs once: it marks itself ended, halts every request (`api/http.ts`), stops the live connection, cancels and clears Query, then leaves the document. C1.8 inserts its unsynced-edits step before the halt.
   - Nothing user-specific is kept in browser storage until C1.8. sessionStorage holds only four `hearth:*` keys (why the last document left, a return path from `APP_PATHS`, a reload stamp and a focus flag), the router's scroll offsets keyed by path, and the router's own one-shot reload marker. WS-01 keeps every other file off sessionStorage, and the app off localStorage, IndexedDB and Cache Storage.
   - **The boot order.** `index.html` loads one module, `src/main.ts`, whose static import graph holds only CSS and modules that import no package. It runs `preBoot()`, which removes the router's scroll cache unconditionally, reloads a page restored from the back-forward cache, turns a failed chunk into a reload at most once a minute, and sets zod's `jitless` before zod loads, so zod never probes `new Function` under the CSP. Then one dynamic import loads the app. By ES module semantics nothing in that import's graph, router-core included, evaluates before the call, whatever the bundler does with chunks. **A chunking change (`manualChunks`, `advancedChunks`, a vendor split) or a new static import in `main.ts` must keep that rule**: WS-10 checks the source and SH-12 the built bundle.
   - Scroll positions are restored within a document, never across a reload. C1 may revisit this with a per-person key.
3. **Data.** One QueryClient per signed-in document (`session/query-client.ts`; WS-02 keeps `new QueryClient(` there).
   - A query retries once on 409 `try_again` and up to twice on a 5xx or no answer; a mutation retries once on `try_again` only, since any other failed write may have landed. Each wait is full jitter, doubling from 1 s to at most 30 s. A `signed_out` refusal from any query or mutation ends the session. From C0.9 PR 2, a 403 `notice_required` from any query or mutation is never retried: it sends the tab to `/notice`, whose own read decides (T22 item 31).
   - Topic-backed queries use the key `['topic', <topic>]`, which the live connection invalidates or removes; C1 adds the first.
   - `GET /api/session` is refetched at most once a minute. The flags are polled every 60 s while the page is visible, on focus and on the gateway's nudge (T17 item 11), which is how C0.8 reads "every 60 s".
   - Every call is a relative `/api` path with same-origin credentials, and only `api/http.ts` calls `fetch` (WS-03).
4. **Gateway client policy** (`apps/web/src/live/gateway-client.ts`, `CLIENT_POLICY`; the client's side of `packages/shared/src/gateway.ts` items 1 to 7).
   - Limits come from each `welcome`, capped by `GATEWAY_LIMITS`: a welcome can lower them, never raise them. Until the first welcome the client keeps a 25 s heartbeat and a 30 s spread.
   - No welcome within 15 s of opening, or no open within 30 s of connecting, is a failed try.
   - Its own token bucket (18 frames, refilling 4 a second) stays under the server's 20 and 5, so the server's never empties.
   - "Reconnecting…" shows once the client has failed to reconnect for 30 s, and from then it asks `GET /api/flags` whether the session still holds (T19 item 11). A probe unanswered after 20 s is let go and its request ended, so a request that hangs can't hold back the next one or stay open until it, and a late answer counts for nothing. 4401 ends the session; 4426 reloads at most once a minute, after which the banner offers Reload; 4403 pauses the connection for the notice. From C0.9 PR 2 the client opens a socket only once the person has acknowledged the current notice: before that it starts in a 4403's pause (`startPaused()`, GW-26), and `resume()`, which the notice store calls after an acknowledgement, resumes only that pause, never a 4426's (T22 item 31). While 4426 or 4403 holds the connection paused, nothing would hear a 4401, so the client asks `GET /api/flags` at a random point in the first 30 s and every 30 s after, and a 401 ends the session as it does when stalled.
   - In C0.8 the runtime holds the person's own topic, so the subscribe path runs end to end.
   - **Known limit:** realtime takes at most 8 sockets per session (`SOCKETS_PER_SESSION`), so a 9th tab is refused at the upgrade, which the browser sees as a plain 1006. It shows "Reconnecting…" and keeps retrying within the back-off. A socket shared across tabs is the fix if real use needs it.
5. **Navigation** (`apps/web/src/shell/nav.ts`, `router/router.tsx`).
   - Five tabs on a phone: Today, Chats, Rooms, Tasks and More (OD-02). A rail from 48rem wide **and** 30rem tall holds Today, Chats, Rooms, Tasks, Calendar and Drive, so a phone on its side keeps the tab bar. More is a page, listing Calendar and Drive.
   - `/` lands on Chats (OD-03). `/sign-in` sits outside the shell and sends a signed-in document to Chats. A signed-out visit to an app path keeps it to return to after sign-in, and only a path in `APP_PATHS`. Every other path shows "Page not found" inside the shell.
   - No route takes a search parameter, so no URL carries state.
   - Reserved, and not a route yet: `/admin/*` (C0.16). C0.9 PR 2 added `/notice`, outside the shell like `/sign-in`, and the settings registry's pages, `/settings` and `/settings/privacy`, in it (T22 item 31).
   - Pages that no navigation lists (C0.9): the DPO's `/dpo/purposes`, `/dpo/transfers` and, from PR 2, `/dpo/notice` (T22 items 15 and 31) are routes in the shell, reached from the avatar menu, until C0.16 moves them under `/admin`; so are `/settings` and `/settings/privacy`. So `router/paths.ts` keeps two lists: `NAV_PATHS`, the places the tab bar, More and the rail reach, which NV-01 holds to both navigations; and `APP_PATHS`, every shell path (`NAV_PATHS`, the settings pages and the DPO pages), which RO-01 holds equal to the routes the shell registers and which the return path through sign-in keeps to.
   - The acknowledgement gate (C0.9 PR 2, T22 item 31): the shell's guard sends a document whose person hasn't acknowledged the current notice to `/notice`, keeping the path to return to (`noticeAllowsPath`): every path once acknowledged, `/dpo/*` for the DPO before any version, and nothing else. `/notice` offers no Skip, and Sign out.
   - No AI, Ask, assistant or agent entry, ever, and no AI copy on any screen until an A-stage turns one on (OD-06, BUILD-PLAN decision 8; `strings/ai-copy.ts`).
   - The avatar menu is a registry (OD-05); each step adds its own entry, which may say when it shows. C0.9 added the first, "Data protection", above Sign out, shown only when `GET /api/session` answers `capabilities.registers` true (item 10), and its PR 2 put "Settings" first, so the menu is Settings, Data protection (when shown), Sign out (C0.9 PD-47).
   - The Search bar, labelled "Search", is built and mounted nowhere until C1.7 (OD-04).
   - The app-wide states (`screens/`): an unknown path is "Page not found" inside the shell, with a way to Chats; a screen that fails keeps the shell and its h1, says so, and offers Try again (a new document when the screen's code failed to load) and Go to Chats; and a boot that can't learn who is signed in, its `GET /api/session` failing or unanswered after 15 s, shows "Can't reach Hearth" alone, with Try again. The live connection's line is item 4's.
6. **Design system.**
   - **The look is the-newnormal/finance's** (owner, 21 Oct 2026): its canvas `#f7f5f1` and white surfaces; its blue-tinted ink `#34363e`; brand-deep `#2f7f9b` for the primary fill, the focus ring and the Switch, and brand `#60aecb` on inverse grounds; danger `#b4443a`; lavender `#c9c0ea` behind selected text; Inter for text and Newsreader 600 for headings; pill buttons (the main action 48 px tall), 12 px fields, 16 px cards, menus and the segmented control's track, 24 px dialogs and the sign-in card; 1 px hairlines and its two soft shadows; selected states in ink (`bg-fg text-surface`, its `bg-ink text-white`); alerts with a 1 px edge of their tone and a light tint; and its teal and lavender wash behind the shell and the sign-in screen (the `wash` utility in `app.css`). `demo/` stays the reference for behaviour, not for the look. Hearth keeps its own flame mark on the accent: white on brand-deep in light (the favicon too) and dark ink on brand in dark; newnormal's logo is not copied into this public repository.
   - Tokens on `:root` with one `prefers-color-scheme` block: the system theme only, no toggle (OD-08). Where a finance colour fails a Hearth rule, the nearest value that passes takes its place, most of them finance's own email colours: `--muted` `#67696f` (finance's ink-soft is translucent, every token is a solid `#rrggbb`, and its email value `#6b6d74` is 4.38:1 on `--surface-2`), `--line-strong` `#82838a` for a border that identifies a control at 3:1 (finance's field border is its 1.24:1 hairline), `--accent-ink` `#276d85` (finance's own button hover) for accent text, and agent, ok and warn from its email pills. `--surface-2` `#efece6` (near finance's ink/5 hover on canvas, `#edebe8`) and `--wall` are Hearth's own grounds, which finance doesn't need. The ink tokens for text on filled colours, the inverse accent and the twelve avatar fills, each 4.5:1 under its ink (X8, QA-01), stay. `styles/contrast.test.ts` holds every pair the kit uses to its WCAG minimum in both themes, with the wash's two brightest points among the grounds, and TK-13 (`app-css.test.ts`) holds the wash to what those pairs cover.
   - **Defaults for the owner to confirm** in the PR that took finance's look, each the option closest to finance that Hearth's rules allow:
     - **The dark palette** is Hearth's own, since finance has none: grounds from finance's ink (`#1a1b20`, `#222329`, `#2b2c33`), text `#f2efe9`, brand `#60aecb` as the accent with dark ink on it (white on brand is 2.50:1), lavender for agents, finance's orange for warnings, and a lighter red `#ea7a6e` with dark ink.
     - **The wash is 25 % of teal and 23 % of lavender in light, and 13 % of each in dark** (finance's is 70 % at its centre, blurred): near the strongest at which muted, error and accent text and control borders still pass on it. The lavender is a step under 25 % (`#ece9ef`, where 25 % is `#ece8ef`), so a pixel a renderer draws one 8-bit step past a wash token, as Chromium was seen to, still keeps muted and error text at 4.51:1 or more (on the lavender token itself they are 4.56:1, where 25 % gave 4.53). It is opaque, and no surface is see-through or blurred: nothing scrolls under Hearth's bars or the sign-in card, so finance's 75 % and 70 % veils would add a tint of at most 1.03:1 under the bars and 1.06:1 under the sign-in card, at the wash's brightest point, in light.
     - **The current tab is finance's ink pill**, its icon in surface ink, with a heavier label. An accent-soft pill would be 1.06:1 on the bar.
     - **warn is finance's warning tone** (`#9a4a17` on `#fdeee3`, its "not approved" pill), not its yellow attention tint, which a later stage can add as a token of its own.
     - **Selected text sits on finance's lavender**, with its ink set to `--fg`, since muted and error text on lavender are 3.19:1.
     - **Alerts are finance's**: a 1 px edge of the tone at 30 % and a light tint; the error tint is finance's 5 % danger on white (`#fbf6f5`), and the info tint its email info pill's ground (`#e3f1f6`, `--accent-soft`), one token for the info alert, the Updated banner and C1's info pill, edged in the accent (finance's info alert is its teal at 20 %, about `#eef7f8` on white, edged in its brand `#60aecb`). The sign-in steps' two notes also carry an icon (the error notice in its red, the new-code note in ink), so the error isn't told by colour alone (WCAG 1.4.1); finance's alerts have none, and nor do the connection banners.
     - **Segmented is finance's large control** (a hairline-edged surface track with `p-1.5` and `gap-2`, `rounded-xl` segments with `px-4 py-2.5`, the chosen one in ink), at least 44 px tall.
     - **The screen's h1 is 24 px on a phone and 36 px from the rail breakpoint** (finance's page title is 30 and 36 px). The phone's header row never scrolls away and holds Back and the avatar menu, so at 320 px with 200 % text the h1 has 152 px, where "Today" and "Rooms" at 30 px (157 and 176 px) would break mid-word. From the rail breakpoint the title sits where finance's does: 40 px under the window's top edge (its `sm:pt-10`; Hearth has no top bar there), 24 px above the content (its `mb-6`: the header's 4 px and `Page`'s 20), and in `Page`'s wide column, so it starts where the content does (`RAIL_TITLE_ROW` in `ui/ScreenHeader.tsx`, KM-05).
     - **Kept from Hearth**: body text at 15 px (finance works at 14 px), inputs at 16 px and targets at 44 px (`CLAUDE.md`), the field's hint above the field (it is read before the field and stays above a phone's keyboard; finance's is below), a red border on a field with an error, and `lang="en-GB"`. Also:
       - **the sign-in steps' frame** (`sign-in/StepFrame.tsx`): on a phone no card, with the mark, the h1 and the step's words at the top and the form at the bottom, in the thumb's reach (PRD-66); from the rail breakpoint the same column as a 448 px card (`max-w-md`) with the mark inside it at the top left, so the reading order is the same at every width. Finance centres a 384 px card at every width, its logo above it and a footnote below;
       - **empty states** (`ui/EmptyState.tsx`) keep their icon and a 20 px Newsreader h2: in C0.8 an empty state is a screen's whole content, under its h1. Finance's are a line of small muted text inside a card;
       - **the titles of the screens outside the shell** (`screens/PlainScreen.tsx`: the boot error, and not found and the error screen when they render outside the shell) are all 30 px, finance's error page size, where its not-found page is 36 px: at 320 px with 200 % text, "Something" in "Something went wrong" is already wider than the column at 30 px, and at 36 px it would be wider than the screen.
   - Type: body 15 px, small 13 px, and the smallest 12 px (`text-xs`: the tab labels and a small avatar's initials). Inter at 400 and 500 (buttons, labels, menu items), and 600 only for the current tab's label and avatar initials; Newsreader at 600 upright only (WS-14 holds every string, and every `className` or `cx()` read whole with the same-file constants and functions it names, that sets `font-display` to `font-semibold` at every width and state, with no other weight and no italic), for headings, never for body text, labels or buttons.
   - Colours only through the tokens (WS-04), no opacity on text (WS-05), 44 px targets at every width, 16 px inputs.
   - Dates and times are day first on a 12-hour clock ('Sun 11 Oct 2026', '2:30 pm'), in the viewer's zone: their profile's, else the org's (OD-10; `lib/time.ts`).
   - The layout has no fixed bars (D-08): a `100dvh` grid with one scroll container, and the screen's one h1 in flow at the top of `<main>`, never scrolled away, so a bar never hides focus (WCAG 2.4.11). The scroll container takes a tab stop of its own only while it scrolls with nothing in the tab order (`ui/scroller-focus.ts`, as Chromium does for any scroller; WCAG 2.1.1), and is then a region named by the screen's h1 (`aria-labelledby`, WCAG 4.1.2), so a keyboard can scroll a screen of text, such as Settings › Privacy at 320 px (C0.9 PR 3a), and every other screen's tab order is its content's alone.
   - The kit is native-first (D-03): Dialog is `<dialog>` with `showModal()`, Segmented a fieldset of radios, Switch a checkbox with `role="switch"`, Select a native select, and forms are `noValidate` and handled by `onSubmit`. Only Menu and the toast store are hand-rolled.
   - The demo's Card, UnreadBadge, Pill, AvatarStack, SectionTitle and RecordingIndicator wait for their first user (D18), which ports them with their review fixes, in finance's look (a Pill's tones: info `accent-soft`, success `ok-soft`, warning `warn-soft`, agents `agent-soft`): Card is a `<button type="button">` when clickable (SHL-47, C1); UnreadBadge reads to screen readers as "{n} unread", its visible number hidden from them and shown as `99+` above 99 (CHT-40, C1.2); RecordingIndicator sits inside a landmark (Principle 6, QA-10, C2.3); Pill, AvatarStack and SectionTitle come with C1.
7. **CSP and headers** (`documentHeaders()`, `packages/shared/src/web-headers.ts`; D-04). Production's policy:

   `default-src 'none'; script-src 'self'; style-src 'self'; img-src 'self'; font-src 'self'; connect-src 'self' wss://<host>; base-uri 'none'; form-action 'none'; frame-ancestors 'none'; object-src 'none'; report-uri /api/csp-report`

   - With it: `Cross-Origin-Opener-Policy: same-origin`, `Referrer-Policy: same-origin` (no other origin gets a Referer either way, and under `no-referrer` the Fetch standard would send every browser's CSP report with `Origin: null`, which the server takes only with `Sec-Fetch-Site: same-origin`, item 9), `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`, `X-Frame-Options: DENY`, `Permissions-Policy: camera=(), microphone=(), geolocation=()`, and HSTS for an https host other than this machine.
   - Vite's dev server serves it relaxed only by `'unsafe-inline'` on `script-src` and `style-src`; `connect-src` stays the allow-list there too.
   - Every source derives from the app's own origin, so no CDN or model host can enter it. The gateway's `wss://` origin is named because older WebKit's reading of `'self'` for it is unverified.
   - Left out on purpose: nonces and `'strict-dynamic'` (a static app has no per-request nonce); Trusted Types (Chromium only); `report-to`, which silences `report-uri` where both are set; `upgrade-insecure-requests`, which would break the http dev server; `manifest-src` and `worker-src` (C0.14); and the user-content origin (C0.15, C1.5). `form-action 'none'`: no form submits natively.
   - `scripts/guards/csp.test.ts` parses the policy against its own allow-list and CloudFront's 1,783-character limit, holds `index.html` to nothing inline, and keeps Vite's dev server and the e2e preview on the builder.
8. **Hosting contract** (OD-11; for C0.11 and C0.12, and C0a's minimal non-prod deploy from the start):
   - CloudFront with an S3 origin holding `apps/web/dist`; `/api/*` and `/ws` go to the ALB on the same host.
   - `/api/*` and `/ws` use the managed `CachingDisabled` cache policy, so no answer is stored for anyone else, and the `AllViewerExceptHostHeader` origin request policy, so `Origin`, `Sec-Fetch-Site`, cookies, query strings and the upgrade headers reach the ALB. Without `Origin` the root hook refuses every POST, and realtime every upgrade; without `Sec-Fetch-Site` it refuses WebKit's CSP reports (item 9).
   - The app's fallback for its own paths is a viewer-request function on the S3 behaviour only, never a custom error response, which would turn `/api/*` 404s into `index.html`.
   - `index.html` and the files from `public/` are `no-cache`; `/assets/*` is `public, max-age=31536000, immutable`.
   - A deploy uploads the assets before `index.html` and keeps the previous builds' `/assets/*` for 7 days, so open tabs can still load their chunks. That is a deploy step, never an S3 lifecycle rule.
   - The response-headers policy equals `documentHeaders(appOrigin)`. **C0.12 owes a CDK unit test** that asserts it, with the cache and origin policies above.
   - `TRUST_PROXY` holds the load balancer's subnets and CloudFront's origin-facing ranges (T14 item 18), and the same CDK unit test asserts both. The server's own peer is the ALB, so without its subnets every request's address would be the ALB's, and every per-IP limit one shared bucket.
9. **CSP reports** (`POST /api/csp-report`, `apps/server/src/web/csp-report.ts`; OD-12). A browser's report, `application/csp-report` JSON of at most 8 KiB, becomes at most one warn line of four reduced fields: the directive; what was blocked, as a keyword, an origin or a scheme; the page's path, with every segment that isn't one of the app's route words (`ROUTE_WORDS` in `packages/shared/src/web-headers.ts`, which the router test RO-06 holds equal to the words of the registered routes) replaced by `:id`, so a name or slug in a typed or pasted URL is never kept; and the disposition. Everything else is dropped, and nothing is stored. At most 60 lines a minute per process. It takes no session, asks no `can()`, reaches neither Postgres nor Valkey, and stores nothing. The lines reach CloudWatch, which C0.9 lists in PDPA-10.
   - **The Origin hook's one exception** (owner, 21 Oct 2026; `isSameOriginCspReport` in `apps/server/src/origin.ts`). A POST that Fastify matches to this route passes with `Origin: null` when `Sec-Fetch-Site` is exactly `same-origin`. WebKit (Safari, and every browser on iPhone, the pilot's floor) sends a page's CSP report with `Origin: null` whatever the page's referrer policy (`FrameLoader::addHTTPOriginIfNeeded` with no origin, from `PingLoader::sendViolationReport`), so the hook refused every one in CI. From Safari 16.4 it also sends `Sec-Fetch-Site: same-origin` for a report to its own origin. Chromium and Firefox send the page's Origin and pass as any POST from the app does.
   - No page can set `Sec-Fetch-Site`. So a report, form, `fetch` or beacon from another site (`cross-site`) or from the user-content origin (`same-site`) is still refused, as is any Origin but the app's own or `null`, and a missing one. The exception takes no other route or method, and the WebSocket upgrade keeps the exact Origin check. A client that isn't a browser can send both headers, as it can already send the app's Origin to any route; here it reaches a route that takes no session, reads no cookie, stores nothing and logs at most the capped, reduced line. `scripts/guards/csp-report.test.ts` keeps the route that way.
   - A `null` report is logged only when its page is on the app origin (CR-10); a report with the app's Origin is logged whatever its page (CR-11). WebKit sends another site's own reports with `Origin: null` too, but with `Sec-Fetch-Site: cross-site`, so the hook refuses them; only a client that isn't a browser can send one with both headers, and it can't spend the log's allowance.
   - A refusal's warn line (`origin_refused`) names the kind of Origin (`absent`, `null` or `other`) and of `Sec-Fetch-Site`, never another origin's value, so a pilot browser whose reports are still refused shows up in the logs.
10. **`GET /api/session` and `GET /api/flags`** (`apps/server/src/web/`; D-05: no new write and no migration).
    - `GET /api/session` answers `{ personId, name, timeZone, capabilities }`: the profile's display name and zone, else the roster's name and the org's zone, and from C0.9 capability booleans that `can()` decides, never roles. The first is `registers` (T22 item 14): whether `can()` allows the person `register.read` once re-authenticated, which shows the avatar menu's "Data protection"; the server still decides every request to the registers. The client's lenient reading takes a missing or unreadable capability as false. No number and no roles; C0.16 adds more capabilities as its screens need them. From C0.9 PR 2 it also answers `notice`, the person's standing with the current notice (`acknowledged`, `required` or `unpublished`, T22 item 28), which the shell reads before it starts the gateway; the client reads a missing or unreadable standing as `required`.
    - `GET /api/flags` answers `{ flags }` (T17 item 11). No production flag is `client: true` yet (OD-13), so in production that is `{ flags: {} }`.
    - One access action decides both, `session.read_own`, for any active person and without re-authentication, in a read-only transaction. It is exempt from the acknowledgement gate, so both answer before the person has acknowledged the notice (T22 item 28). The flags' subject comes from the `World` that `can()` decided on. Its one refusal, someone who has left (a deactivation that commits after the acting session was re-read), answers 401 `signed_out` like an ended session, never 404.
    - The session GET counts as use of the session (the existing after-response touch, exempt from the outbox). The flags GET doesn't touch it (OD-14), so a polling tab can't keep an idle session alive. Neither answers a revision.
    - **A GET never clears the session cookie:** their 401 carries no `Set-Cookie`, so a boot's 401 can't delete a session another tab has just made. From C0.9 PR 2 `GET /api/notice`, which `/notice` polls, is registered through the same `keepCookie` (T22 item 26). Clearing stays with sign-out and the sign-in steps. The older GETs (your devices, the admin device list) still clear; C0.16 aligns them.
11. **Tests.**
    - Playwright specs (`apps/web/e2e/*.spec.ts`) run only through `npm run e2e`, never in `npm test` or the integration run (TM-SPEC).
    - The e2e run (D-06): the built app over Vite's preview, served over HTTPS on `https://localhost:5180` with a throwaway self-signed certificate and production's headers; the api and realtime roles from source on 3210 and 3211, on their own database, `hearth_e2e`, which every run rebuilds (`HEARTH_DB=hearth_e2e scripts/db-local.sh reset`), so the suite never touches `hearth`; `DEPLOY_ENV=test`, Valkey DB 9 flushed every run, and `SMS_PROVIDER=fake`, whose stderr line the specs read. The preview refuses a build older than its sources.
    - The personas (`e2e/support/personas.ts`) budget every code request against PRD-53's windows: no number asked for more than twice a run, at most 25 requests a run, and no number shared by two projects that run at once.
    - Browsers (OD-07, OD-16): Chromium runs every spec and gates; WebKit and Firefox run the `@smoke` cases, CS-03 among them, so each engine's own CSP report is shown to reach `/api/csp-report` (item 9); `chromium-session-end`, which ends the sessions the others share, runs after them all.
