67. **The recovery mode (PD-127, PD-128).** Item 44's contract, clause by clause: it runs non-interactively, in one transaction, acting as the org's Hearth principal (R1 to R5); it connects with a 30 s `transaction_timeout` as a start-up option (item 65); it sets the marker and the 2 s `lock_timeout` at the transaction's start (R2); it passes every fact from the snapshot, taking from the person only the review date and the reason unless the DPO decided a stated fact changed (R7); it prints the clearance and `transfer_send_state('twilio')` (R8, item 69); and it meets BSC-04 and BSC-05 (BSS-07 to BSS-12).
    - **R1** begins as S1 does. **R2** sets `hearth.break_glass` to `twilio_reclear` and the 2 s `lock_timeout`, transaction-local, with the server's facts: the only code outside tests and migrations that sets the marker (BSG-04). **R3** reads exactly one org (D1), else rolls back and exits 1 ("This database has no organisation" / "This database has more than one organisation: stop, and tell the DPO and the Owners."), the second cross-org read, of org ids only (PD-136); R6 reads that org's zone by its id. **R4** its one Hearth principal. **R5** `app.org_id` and `app.user_id`.
    - **No advisory lock (PD-124),** neither the bootstrap's nor Kysely's: item 44's own locks serialise two re-clears and a re-clear against the DPO's clear (BGR-03, BGR-04), a re-clear creates no org a backfill could miss, and holding a deploy's migration for up to a minute in an emergency gains nothing. The runbook pauses the deploy pipeline for every mode, and a stale checkout is still refused by the ledger comparison.
    - **R6,** one plain read: how many current DPOs hold a live session and when the last of those sessions ends (item 44 check 7's predicate, word for word from 0011, so the count means what the function will decide); Twilio Verify's open clearance (its id, how and on which day it was cleared), or else the day its last one ended, or that it was never cleared, read plainly as CLAUDE.md asks of a read that only shows whether a provider is cleared; and the org's zone, read by the org's id (R3 reads ids only), with the review date's distance in it. These print at once, with the inputs repeated back, before the function runs, so a plan the function then refuses still says how many DPOs are signed in and until when, and a plan run after a lost commit shows whether that commit's clearance is the open one. A plain read takes no lock, so it adds nothing ahead of the function's people rows in the lock order; the function's locked check decides, and a session starting or ending in that instant can make the two differ (BSR-04).
    - **R7, one statement that maps the snapshot (PD-127).** It reads the facts snapshot of the Twilio clearance the latest version links (`notice_version_providers`), else the last ended Twilio clearance's; overlays the DPO's record, the entry's current facts, only for the `fromEntry` keys and only through `notice_facts()`, which keeps exactly the seven stated facts (check 6 holds it equal to `NOTICE_TRANSFER_FACTS`), so even a key that slipped past the input check can overlay nothing else; and calls `bootstrap_transfer_clearance('twilio', …)` with all 18 arguments mapped inside SQL, as 155's helper maps them, the review date always the person's and never the snapshot's. So no date, array, boolean or null is converted in TypeScript, and no fact is parsed, mapped or printed by the process. The linked snapshot comes first because a "Save and keep cleared" that changed a stated fact ends one clearance and opens another, so the last ended one can hold facts the notice doesn't state (PD-78); a stand-in latest version links nothing and falls back (production never has one, PD-33). With no snapshot at all the function refuses in its own words: check 1 with no clearance ever, check 8 with one open. A named key whose value already matches changes nothing, and is no refusal. The statement also returns the entry's facts as they stood before the call, as opaque text that crosses the process once, never parsed, logged or printed. Plain reads are enough: snapshots and versions never change, and a publish or a DPO's Save in between needs a live DPO session, which the function refuses after taking every DPO's row.
    - **R8 (PD-128)** reads what to print: the clearance, how it was cleared, its review date, the names of the facts that moved in the entry (comparing the entry before, R7's text, with after; never their values, and never read from `audit_events`, which CLAUDE.md reads only through `read_audit_events()`) and `transfer_send_state('twilio')`. BSS-07 and BSS-10 prove the printed names equal the audit row's `changed` keys. **R9** `commit`, after "committing the re-clear of Twilio Verify, clearance <id>"; or `rollback`, for a plan.
    - **What a re-clear does to the revision.** 0011 diffs every key of `transfer_register_facts()`, `review_on` included, and moves the revision by one with Hearth as `updated_by` whenever any differs (item 44, "What it records"). So: the stated snapshot with the review date the entry already holds keeps the revision, `changed` empty; with a new review date, the usual case, `changed` holds `review_on` alone, the revision moves, and the send state still reads `cleared`, since `review_on` is no notice fact; a fact the DPO's own Save left different from the snapshot, not named in `fromEntry`, goes back to the snapshot's value, recorded from and to. The DPO names a stated fact they meant to keep (and the send state then reads `unstated`), and records an unstated one again afterwards (the runbook's Afterwards). The Transfers page then shows the entry as set by Hearth beside "Cleared again by the emergency re-clear".
    - **How a lost commit is read.** The plan's `Twilio Verify now` line: the clearance id the "committing" line named, cleared by `break_glass`, means it committed; `not cleared` means it didn't, and the same command with `--commit` re-clears. The function's "cleared already" can't tell, since 0011 checks for a live DPO session first, so a DPO who signed in since would make the rerun answer that instead.
    - **Calling `transfer_send_state()` here** (and in the set-up's S15) is a named exception to CLAUDE.md's "Only that deciding transaction calls either" (PD-128): item 44 asks for it, the call reads only the clearance this transaction wrote, the state is printed only once COMMIT returns (a plan prints "would leave the send state … at"), and computing it in TypeScript would copy 0010's rule and drift from it.
    - **What the person supplies:** the DPO's id, the review date, the reason and, if the DPO decided so, which stated facts come from their record. Never a fact's value, never the provider (fixed to `twilio`, with item 44 check 2 behind it), never the org (found, D1).
68. **The correction (OD-29; PD-129).** The one repair a wrong number needs before anyone can sign in, and nothing more. It is open only while all of these hold, each checked under the locks below: the database has one org; the org has one person, people who have left included, holding `owner` and `dpo` granted by Hearth (the founder); that person has no `users` row, so has never signed in and has no device or session (both reference `users`, 0003); and the org has no notice version.
    - **C1** begins as S1 does. **C2** is S2: the marker `''`, the lock bound, the server's facts, and the log settings refused as the set-up refuses them, since it binds the number. **C3** both advisory locks, alone in their statement. **C4 to C6** the one org with the ledger read again, as S4 reads it, its Hearth principal, and `app.org_id` and `app.user_id`. **C7** the roster `FOR NO KEY UPDATE`, exactly one row, as CLAUDE.md asks of anything that may end a person's access: a sign-in step holding it `FOR SHARE` finishes first and is then seen, and one that starts later waits. **C8,** plain reads once that row is held: their two role rows, whether a `users` row exists, whether a notice version exists, and whether the new number equals their current one, compared in SQL so the old number never reaches the process. **C9** the old number removed by Hearth, never overwritten (0002: "A changed number is removed, not overwritten"). **C10** the new number, current, by Hearth. **C11** one audit row, `bootstrap.correct_number`, `details` `{"by": "bootstrap", "from": <the old phone_numbers id>, "to": <the new id>}`, never a number. **C12** the new number current for the founder alone and the old one for nobody, else rollback. **C13** `commit`, after "committing the correction"; or `rollback`, for a plan.
    - **Refusals, each writing nothing (exit 1):** nobody on the roster; someone other than the founder on it ("Someone other than the founder is on the roster, so the set-up's number can't be corrected here."); the one person not the founder; "The founder has signed in, so their number can't be corrected here. C0.16's phone change does it (ACC-06)."; "The first version of the notice is published, so the set-up's number can't be corrected here."; no current number; and "The founder's number is already that one. Nothing to correct.", which is also how a retry of a committed correction reads (item 69).
    - **Lock order:** the two advisory locks, then people → users (read) → phone_numbers, the order's own. A sign-in's finish reads the number `FOR SHARE` after the person, and a code request's `person_by_phone()` reads it `FOR SHARE`, which C9 makes wait and then re-read as removed (BSR-03).
    - **Why so narrow.** With no account row nothing references the number but the roster, and no session, device, PIN or socket exists to end, so the correction needs no `signin.ended`, no `pin_reset` and no revocation. Once the founder, or anyone holding the number, has signed in, C0.16's phone change, with its identity check and its ending of every session (T20 item 14.4), is the path; a wider window would override an account someone has used. No event: `person.phone_changed` is reserved for C0.16. The DPO writes the correction document into the bootstrap secret as a new version, the person on call loads it as for the set-up, and the runbook allows it only at the founder's own request, with them present, after their code didn't arrive.
69. **Output, exit statuses, locks and bounds (PD-125, PD-131, PD-132).**
    - **One printer (PD-131).** Only `output.ts` writes to stdout or stderr (BSG-03), and only typed lines, each field checked by a zod schema of ids, enums, days, instants, counts, setting and migration names, the masked number, the org's name and, on one line, the founder's name; each line is prefixed `owner-bootstrap:`, results to stdout and refusals to stderr. The lines: `target`, every run (the mode, plan or commit, the database, `DEPLOY_ENV`, the input's environment, the marker, how many migrations are applied and the newest, and the bounds); in a set-up plan, `organisation`, `founder_name`, `number` and `reviews` (both review dates); in a correction plan, `number`; in every recovery run, `inputs` (the named DPO, the review date and how many days ahead it is in the org's zone, the stated facts taken from the DPO's record, and the reason's length, never its text), `dpo_sessions` (the count and, if any, when the last session ends, in the org's zone and then UTC), `twilio_now`, `facts_source` and `changed`, and `log_warning` where item 65 says; then `committing`, `committed` with the ids, `send_state`, `next` after a set-up, and `kept_nothing` after every plan.
    - **Where each value appears.** The org's name, the masked number (`maskNumber()`: the country code and the last two digits, never the whole number and never a digest of it, which with the name would narrow it to a few) and the review dates only in plans. The founder's full name only in a set-up plan whose stdout is a terminal, so they check its spelling with the person on call; otherwise only its length, so it never reaches a pipe, a file or a one-off task's log. Never printed: the number in any other form, the reason, any fact's value, the connection string, the host, the user, a password or `OWNER_BOOTSTRAP_TLS_NAME`, R7's earlier facts, or an error's `detail`, `hint`, `where`, `schema`, `table`, `column` or `routine`. A `DatabaseError` is read only for its code and constraint, and for its message only under Hearth's own fixed-text SQLSTATEs (`HF005` to `HF008`, and `42501`, `22023` and `23514` raised by a Hearth trigger or function, whose messages `twilio-withdrawn.md` acts on, with `55P03` and `40P01` worded by the script); anything else prints "Postgres refused it (SQLSTATE …, constraint …)", since Postgres's own messages can quote input. Process-level handlers for an uncaught error and an unhandled rejection send it through the same sanitiser, so Node's own print, which would show a `DatabaseError`'s detail, never runs.
    - **Exit statuses (PD-132),** each tied to a runbook step: 0, done or the plan passed; 1, refused by Hearth's rules (the once rule, the function, a verification), nothing written; 2, the inputs or the target are wrong, or connecting failed (anything before the first statement returns, a client pg refuses to make included, which `openClient()` catches like any other failure to connect), with one fixed `could not connect:` line naming the cause (the host's name not found, nothing answering, a timeout, an untrusted certificate, a certificate that doesn't name the host, no TLS offered, signing in failed, no such database, a login that may not act as `hearth_owner`, a read-only server, or else the bare code), never the host, the user, the database or Postgres's message, and never "run it again"; 3, once connected, a lock busy, a deadlock, or the connection lost or the run stopped before "committing", nothing written; 4, lost or stopped after "committing", with the mode's own next step (the set-up: run it again, which never sets up twice and names the organisation if this one committed; the re-clear: run the plan and read its `Twilio Verify now` line; the correction: run the plan, where "already that one" means it committed). SIGINT, SIGTERM and SIGHUP print their own `interrupted` line, never the lost-connection advice, and anything outside the closed set prints an `unexpected` line that exits 3 or 4 by phase.
    - **Bounds (PD-125).** Every transaction, in every mode, the set-up's too, whose function path sets none (BSC-04), lasts at most 30 s from its first statement and waits at most 2 s for a lock (start-up options, with `lock_timeout` set again transaction-local at the start, as item 44 asks), and the session ends 5 s after the process stops sending, so a suspended terminal never holds the advisory lock, the DPOs' rows or the outbox's horizon (T16 item 2). The re-clear's function restarts its timer at 30 s, so a re-clear lasts at most about 60 s in all.
    - **Lock order:** the `hearth.owner_bootstrap` advisory lock, then Kysely's migration lock (set-up and correction only), in a statement of their own before any row; then CLAUDE.md's order as items 66 to 68 list; the recovery mode's own reads take no row lock before the function's.
    - **It never** calls Twilio, AWS or any provider, or opens any connection but Postgres (BSG-02); writes a notice, a contact, an acknowledgement, a `users` row, a PIN, a device or a session; grants a role or adds a person beyond the set-up's; sets the marker outside `recover`; sends `COMMENT ON`, `ALTER DATABASE`, `ALTER ROLE` or `ALTER SYSTEM`, or a setting that outlives its transaction (BSG-06); publishes to Valkey; reads `audit_events`; runs against migrations other than the checkout's or an unexpected target; or takes a value from an argument, a file or a prompt.
70. **What CI checks (PD-133, PD-134).** Every value is fictional: names such as "Wren Example", numbers only from Ofcom's drama range, facts as the harness and 155's helpers give them; never the seed's personas. Nothing calls Twilio: the script can't (BSG-02), and the integration suite uses `FakeVerify`. Test names cite their ids.
    - **Unit (BSU-01 to BSU-12, in `npm test`):** `orgNameProblems()` (`packages/shared/src/org-name.test.ts`, BSU-01), and `TransferFactsBody` reading facts as `RecordTransferBody` does (an RGS-14 case in `registers.test.ts`); beside the script, in `scripts/owner-bootstrap/`: the documents, the arguments, the fiction rule, the input's `environment` and `fromEntry`, and refusals that print no input (`input.test.ts`, BSU-02 to BSU-05); the target's matrix, the login's shape, the log settings and the migrations both ways, and the client's settings (`target.test.ts`, BSU-06 and BSU-09); the printer, each line and the process handlers, and the exit-status map with every `could not connect:` line (`output.test.ts`, BSU-07 and BSU-10); and each mode's statements in order on a fake client, the marker, the advisory locks alone in their statement, every founder value a parameter, the recovery statements as text (no `audit_events`, 18 arguments, the review date always the person's, the DPO's record only through `notice_facts()`), and Kysely's lock id equal to the installed Kysely's (`steps.test.ts`, BSU-08, BSU-11 and BSU-12).
    - **The check, `apps/server/scripts/check-owner-bootstrap.ts` (BSS-01 to BSS-16),** a step in CI's `migrations` job after `node scripts/check-access-sql.ts` and before `npm run db:types`. It runs the real script as a child process, as a throwaway NOINHERIT login whose only membership is `hearth_owner`, against copies of a template it builds with production's migrator and gives write tracking, on this machine only. Each mode's plan keeps nothing; the set-up writes BSC-02's rows and the server reads them as it will (`withOrg({ system: true })`'s lookup, `personByPhone()`, the roles, `transfer_send_state()` reading `no_notice`, `codeMaySend()`), and writes exactly its write set in one transaction as `hearth_owner`, with no reserved event; it refuses before connecting (on a closed port) and after (the seed's org, a second run, `--database` over `PGDATABASE`, no TLS under `prod`, the ledger either way, the login's shape, the marker, a server that looks like RDS), and a failure part-way rolls back whole; the bounds hold over longer role defaults; the re-clear runs on the linked snapshot, over the last ended one, with no published version and with a stated fact from the DPO's record, refuses as the function does and keeps its 2 s lock bound; the correction replaces only a number nobody has signed in with; a connection lost at the commit tells the truth; a database default can't make a set-up a re-clear; and no output, audit, outbox or register column holds an input value. In the same check, the races (BSR-01 to BSR-05): two set-ups at once leave one org; a plan and a commit; a correction against the founder's PIN step, each order; a re-clear against a DPO's sign-in, each order, the function's locked check deciding; and a set-up against a deploy's migration, each way, with a ledger row added while it waits. The mutations tried once in the build, each turning a case red, are listed in the PR.
    - **Integration:** the `first-owner` suite (`apps/server/src/auth/first-owner.integration.test.ts`, BSI-01 to BSI-03), whose org the script sets up: `startStack({ firstOrg: 'bootstrap' })` runs `set-up --commit` after the stack's migrations and logins and before write tracking starts, so the sweep sees only the suite's own writes, and `'first-owner'` joins `writes.ts`'s suite union. The founder gets a code before v0 and sets a PIN on the code alone; a member's request is audited `skipped_no_notice`, and the registers show Twilio and AWS cleared by the bootstrap; v0 published over the set-up's clearances reads `cleared`, and the member then gets a code. The recovery mode isn't run in a swept suite: XFR-I8 proves the function's re-clear reaching the api, and BSS-07 to BSS-11 that the script passes it the right arguments.
    - **DB test:** BSC-05 in `db/tests/155_transfers.sql` gains the case a recovery run usually makes, the stated snapshot with a new review date: the revision moves with Hearth as `updated_by`, `changed` holds `review_on` alone, and Twilio still reads `cleared`.
    - **The guard, `scripts/guards/owner-bootstrap.test.ts` (BSG-01 to BSG-11),** each rule first proved on planted text: no identifying value in the script, its tests, the check, the harness, the suite or the runbook (RB-03's shapes, which `scripts/guards/identifying.ts` now holds for both guards, Verify's `VE` SIDs among them, adding two shapes of its own (a number written with 00 or after a trunk 0, at least ten digits), and allowing only Ofcom's drama range, written in any of those forms, and, in `input.ts`, the seed's refusal list); the script's inputs and reach (exactly two options, one reader of `process.env`, its few imports, no network but Postgres, and nothing under `apps/` or `packages/` importing it); one printer, and process-level handlers in the entry that send every uncaught error and every SIGINT, SIGTERM or SIGHUP through it; the seam (`bootstrap_transfer_clearance`, `hearth.break_glass`, `twilio_reclear` and `hearth.owner_bootstrap` named among runtime code only in `steps.ts`, `twilio_reclear` only from `recover()`); constant SQL with every value bound; settings that end with their transaction, no `COMMENT ON` or `ALTER`, and no migration or seed that sets a marker; the shared rules imported, not copied; the parse and the target check before the first connect; CI's step and wiring, and runbooks that name only what the script has, load the input only by item 64's two commands, the secret at a pinned version and the recovery document typed once into `doc` for both runs, never a paste and pause the deploy pipeline before the checkout is installed; a server image that can't carry it; and C0.12's bootstrap secret given to the DPO alone, in both plan files and the set-up runbook, with the person on call only loading it into a run (BSG-11). `design-ids.test.ts` holds PD-01 to PD-136 (PD-134).
    - **What stays as it is:** BSC-01 to BSC-07, the function's contract; BGR-01 to BGR-06, its races and timer; XFR-I8 and XFR-I9, the re-clear reaching the api's sends; NTU-08, its behaviour over upgraded data; and check 6, the trigger on `orgs`. BSS and BSR restate them only through the script, and add what only the script decides: what it reads, where it connects, what it writes around the function, what it prints, and that it runs once.
71. **Open points for the owner (the bootstrap PR), residuals and what later steps inherit.** The owner answered OD-28 to OD-32 on 27 Oct 2026, accepting every recommendation and every default, PD-114 to PD-136, as written ("Accept all"):
    - **OD-28 (a):** the sole Owner's break-glass PIN reset is its own PR, before C0.15, as a fourth mode of the script (`reset-pin`) with its own migration and a checked function in the shape of 0011's re-clear (security invoker, granted to nobody, with its own marker), so C0.15's real-phone run has a repair for a forgotten or admin-locked founder PIN and this PR stays free of migrations (PD-130). The cost: one more PR, with its migration read, before C0.15.
    - **OD-29 (a):** the correction (item 68), only while the founder has never signed in and no notice is published, at their own request with them present. Without it, a typo in the number leaves nobody able to sign in and nothing but a by-hand rebuild to fix it.
    - **OD-30 (a):** Qodo's review is no longer a merge gate, in the owner's own words on 27 Oct 2026 ("always merge without qodo from now on"), which CLAUDE.md's Git section records; Qodo's GitHub App is to be uninstalled, after which a small `chore:` PR deletes `.pr_agent.toml`. Until then the file stays, its header corrected, so a review the app still posts never rewrites a PR description and its "Before merging" steps (PD-135); `renovate.json`'s description drops Qodo. CLAUDE.md's "never resolve a CodeRabbit or Qodo thread" stays, since `protect-main.json` still requires every review thread resolved.
    - **OD-31 (a):** an environment that holds a real person's data counts as production for coding agents and logs, which CLAUDE.md's Security section says when C0.11 builds non-prod; non-prod is set up only once it has its own break-glass role, bootstrap login, environment marker and C0.12's parameter group, and each document's `environment` keeps non-prod's secret out of production and the reverse. Until then C0a's exit rows 2, 4 and 7 read from local e2e (item 19).
    - **OD-32 (a):** production runs the script from a checkout of the deployed commit, under the break-glass role, over a network path C0.11 provides. C0.11 may still choose a one-off task from the server image instead, which moves the files under `apps/server/src/db/` with a `db-entry` exception, reverses BSG-10, changes CLAUDE.md's wording and needs the owner's read; the script needs no change to run either way.

    Still open, for C0.12's build: whether every log setting item 65 reads is visible in `pg_settings` to a role without `pg_read_all_settings` on RDS (on Postgres 17 the six core settings are visible to every role, as on the local 17.11 stack; C0.12 confirms it on RDS. A missing core row is refused by name in a set-up or correction and the person stops and reports it. Any grant that would help goes to `hearth_owner`, the role the session acts as, as an `infra/` step, never to the bootstrap's login, which the script refuses with a second membership), and `auto_explain`'s with them (C0.12 also confirms what `shared_preload_libraries` loads); which SQLSTATE a hot standby answers `begin … read write` with (`0A000` or `25006`, both mapped to the read-only line); that C0.12's creation step can set the database's comment acting as `hearth_owner` on RDS (until it does, the script refuses `nonprod` and `prod`, which fails closed); and, only if OD-32 moves the script into the image, whether CloudTrail keeps a one-off task's overrides.

    Accepted residuals, each in the PR: a holder of `hearth_owner`'s credential or C0.11's break-glass role can write any table directly (item 44), and the script exists so nobody needs to; the environment marker is an intent check, not a privilege, since anyone acting as `hearth_owner` can change the comment; a middle-digit typo passes the plan's masked number, which the founder's first sign-in catches while the correction can still repair it; a stranger holding the committed number could sign in first through the enrolment window (T20 item 12), after which the correction refuses and `breach.md` applies, so the runbook has the founder sign in in the same sitting; a homoglyph org name passes `orgNameProblems()`; the founder's full name, shown in a set-up plan on a terminal, and the masked number reach whatever records that terminal (the DPO's name is already public, D35); a plan in production is still a use of the break-glass role, and takes the advisory lock or every DPO's row for under a second; the re-clear can't know why Twilio was withdrawn; until the PIN reset's PR a forgotten or admin-locked founder PIN has no fix, and a deactivated sole DPO can't be named for a re-clear until C0.16's grant; a corrected wrong number stays in `phone_numbers` as a removed row until C0.10's or C2.9's retention; the printed DPO count is read before the function's locks; a re-clear puts unstated facts back to the published snapshot's values when the DPO's own Save had changed them; Database Activity Streams can't be checked from SQL; the recovery mode runs, with a warning, on log settings a set-up would refuse; a deploy's migration during a recovery run isn't locked out; and the printed changed names compare the entry before and after, the function's audit row staying the record.
    - **What later steps inherit,** each also in its BUILD-PLAN row:
      - **The PIN reset's PR (before C0.15):** the `reset-pin` mode with its own migration (OD-28): `bootstrap_pin_reset()`, its own marker, its checks (acting as Hearth, an identity check and a reason, its own bounds, the person `FOR NO KEY UPDATE`, still at the org and an Owner, no other current Owner, a PIN set), and its writes as `resetPin()` writes them, with Hearth the actor, `account.pin_reset` and `signin.ended` so the revocation consumer closes the founder's sockets, and `person.reset_pin` audited; its DB tests, races against sign-in and a runbook section. It grants no role.
      - **C0.11:** how the person on call reaches RDS to run the script, under the logged break-glass role, with `PGHOST` naming the endpoint or a port forward with `OWNER_BOOTSTRAP_TLS_NAME`; the bootstrap's own login (NOINHERIT, never `hearth_owner`, its only memberships `hearth_owner` with SET and without INHERIT or ADMIN, and `rds_iam`, signed in by IAM, usable only under the break-glass role); whether the script moves into the image (OD-32); and OD-31's sentence in CLAUDE.md when it builds non-prod.
      - **C0.12:** the environment marker on each database, set as its owner when it is created; `log_error_verbosity = terse`, with `log_destination = stderr` (RDS's default; `csvlog` and `jsonlog` write a DETAIL whatever the verbosity), `debug_print_plan` never turned on in the parameter group (the script sets it off for its own session, but the api's `person_by_phone` binds a number too), and `auto_explain` not loaded, or `auto_explain.log_min_duration = -1`, on the bootstrap's database while a set-up or a correction runs, beside the parameter settings already planned, and confirm the log settings are visible in `pg_settings` to `hearth_owner` (if not, a grant to `hearth_owner` through an `infra/` step, never to the bootstrap's login); Database Activity Streams off on the bootstrap's database during a set-up or a correction; one bootstrap secret per environment, its document naming that environment, written, checked and corrected in the AWS console by the DPO alone, through access to that one secret and nothing else, and read under the break-glass role only to load it into a run (never by a service), a correction written as a new version, deleted once the founder has signed in; RDS's CA bundle for `NODE_EXTRA_CA_CERTS`.
      - **C0.13:** an alarm on any `bootstrap.*` audit action, beside the one on `details.by = 'break_glass'`.
      - **C0.15:** the PIN reset's PR lands first, so the real-phone run has a repair for the founder's PIN.
      - **C0.16:** `orgNameProblems()` in org settings; ACC-06's phone change after the founder's first sign-in, when the correction refuses; a second Owner and DPO, the best prevention for the re-clear and the PIN reset alike; and its I3 change for PIN roles (T20 item 14.1) must keep the set-up's S11 passing, which BSS-02 and BSI-01 prove on its PR.
      - **C0.17:** a restored database is re-marked before any use; its source's marker refuses the script meanwhile.
      - **C0.19:** the break-glass drill runs the recovery mode's plan in non-prod, which also shows the DPO count, and the quarterly second-person review covers every bootstrap use.

    In production, in order: C0.11 and C0.12, the deploy's migrate step, the set-up's plan and then its commit; the founder signs in at once on their own phone (T20 item 12); then the DPO records the contact and the bases and publishes v0 (`publishing-the-notice.md`, item 37; after PR 3b, PD-84).

## T23: retention, holds, the deletion log and provenance (C0.10), defaults for the owner to confirm or change

The LH ids in this section ("lifecycle and holds") are C0.10's (LH-02): LH-01 to LH-43 and LH-56 to LH-59 are the defaults C0.10 took, each weighed against the alternative its item names; LH-44 to LH-55, LH-60 and LH-61 are the questions only the owner could answer (and, as D35 makes the owner the DPO, the DPO); LH-28 and LH-52 are retired, and stay recorded here with where their work went, so the run has no gap. They are not C0.9's PD or OD ids (T22), C0.8's AQ or Q ids (T20) nor the PRD's decisions, and outside T23 this file writes them as "C0.10 LH-07". `LH-` ends in neither `PD-`, `OD-` nor `AQ-`, so no other id guard reads one. Every LH id that code cites in `apps/`, `packages/`, `scripts/` and `db/` is one this section records, T23 records every one from LH-01 to the last its index row names, with no gap, and code cites none of the id families of the designs these replaced, `RK-`, `LD-`, `LQ-` and `LO-` (`scripts/guards/design-ids.test.ts`, LCG-01). Test ids use the family LC with a letter for the kind: LCD (DB tests), LCR (races), LCU (the upgrade check), LCS (`packages/shared`), LCA (`packages/access`), LCV (server unit and catalogue tests), LCI (integration) and LCG (guards). One T23 covers C0.10's two PRs: PR 1 (migration 0013) wrote every item, PR 2 (migration 0014) built item 14's provenance registry, and the parts C1.11 builds from this design are marked "designed for C1.11".

The owner decided on 28 Oct 2026, accepting every recommendation and every default as written ("Accept all (Recommended)", in the Claude Code session that day; item 20):
- PR 1 is built on the branch `claude/c010-retention` (the owner's choice), not the design's `feat/c010-holds`;
- the DPO or a Compliance Officer alone places, releases and reads legal holds, never on themselves, with a stated reason and no second approver; a hold that no live reader but its target can release is accepted, and C1.11's console marks holds whose placer has left (LH-44 (a) with (i) and (iii); item 10);
- an open request for a copy or a correction holds its requester's data and itself from receipt to closure (LH-45 (a), item 11); the DPO's confirmation of whether the PDPA's offence of disposing of a record to evade a request applies is given before PR 1 merges, in PR 1's "Before merging" steps;
- a refused copy is held through 30 days after the refusal plus a 14-day margin for a review the PDPC notifies late, 44 days in all, or through a PDPC review (LH-46 (a), item 11); the DPO's confirmation of regulation 8's current wording is given before PR 1 merges, in PR 1's "Before merging" steps;
- requests to the DPO are kept 2 years after closure by default, whatever the outcome, adjustable by the DPO from C2.9 between 90 days and 10 years, never indefinitely (LH-47 (a), item 5);
- the recorded exemptions from holds stand: the clean-up of the outbox, jobs and schedule runs; Valkey's sign-in state and counters; a person's own working-hours edit; the two foreign keys that drop a deleted device's link; CloudWatch logs, traces, RUM and CloudTrail; and C0.15's quarantine copies (LH-48, items 6, 9 and 15);
- OD-27's revoke is carried in PR 1, though "the first migration after PR 3b's worker is deployed" hasn't literally happened, since nothing is deployed (LH-49 (a), item 16);
- a derived item is held while any of its sources is held (LH-50 (a), item 14; built in PR 2, 0014);
- after a restore, replay keeps each row's original cause, checks holds for every cause and the period only for a retention deletion, and retries what it kept; every other delete path waits until the DPO and a hold reader confirm what the restore lost is re-entered, and nobody re-applies a release by hand (LH-51 (a), item 7);
- the defaults the PRD doesn't give stand as item 2 lists them (LH-53);
- s22A's "complete and accurate copy" is preserved in C0 by blocking deletion only (LH-54 (a), item 11);
- from C1.1 a hold on a person covers what they authored, created or owned, by its stored creation context, their DMs and group DMs, and the sessions they took part in; a channel or room needs its own hold; a held item looks the same as any other (LH-55 (a), items 10 and 12);
- the place-hold and release-hold action lands with C1.11, as planned (LH-60 (a), item 20);
- an access-request hold keeps the requester's whole person scope until C6.1 can narrow it (LH-61 (a), item 11);
- and every default, LH-01 to LH-43 and LH-56 to LH-59, as the items below record it. LH-28 and LH-52 stay retired.

They are pinned by:
- **DB tests:** `db/tests/180_retention_classes.sql` (LCD-01 to LCD-05), `185_deletion_log.sql` (LCD-06 to LCD-08), `190_lifecycle_guard.sql` (LCD-09 to LCD-13), `192_access_request_holds.sql` (LCD-14 to LCD-18), `194_legal_holds.sql` (LCD-19, LCD-20 and LCD-41), `196_delete_case.sql` (LCD-27 to LCD-30, C0.10's "held items refuse deletion") and `198_lifecycle_isolation.sql` (LCD-09, LCD-13 and LCD-27 under REPEATABLE READ, each refused: HOLDS-03), and from PR 2 `200_provenance.sql` (LCD-34 to LCD-40), 198's refusals of the provenance definers (LCD-36, LCD-38) and 190's DB-06 list naming `provenance_items_complete()`, with `032_worker_roles.sql` (LCD-31), `040_logs_append_only.sql` (LCD-32), `010`, `015`, `020` and `fixtures/two_orgs.sql` (LCD-33, and from PR 2 the provenance tables and the fixture's `ee` and `ef`), `180` and `192` widened in PR 2, and comments in `030` and `175`;
- **Races:** `apps/server/scripts/check-concurrency.ts`, LCR-01 to LCR-04, LCR-10, LCR-11, LCR-15 and LCR-16, and from PR 2 LCR-12 to LCR-14 and LCR-17 to LCR-20;
- **Scripts:** checks 7 and 8 in `apps/server/scripts/check-catalogues.ts`, with their planted cases in `catalogues.test.ts` (LCV-03 to LCV-05); the composite-foreign-key check in `scripts/check-migrations.sh`, with its probes in `scripts/check-schema-digest.sh`; and the upgrade check `scripts/check-lifecycle-upgrade.sh` (LCU-01 to LCU-05, LCU-07 and LCU-08), with the other upgrade checks applying 0013 over their own data (LCU-09); and from PR 2 `scripts/check-access-sql.ts` over the stored registry, and LCU-10 riding the upgrade check, with LCU-04 widened;
- **Unit:** `packages/shared/src/retention.test.ts` (LCS-01 to LCS-08) and LCS-09 in `packages/shared/src/catalogues.test.ts`; `apps/server/src/lifecycle/retention.test.ts` (LCV-01, LCV-02, LCV-06 and LCV-08); and the restaged todos in `packages/access/src/testing/rules/rule-7.ts` (LH-35); and from PR 2 `packages/access/src/provenance.test.ts` (LCA-04), R3-01b (LCA-05) and R7-11 running in the rules suite, and `apps/server/src/access/provenance-rows.test.ts` (LCV-07);
- **Integration:** LCI-01 in `apps/server/src/auth/sign-in.integration.test.ts` and LCI-02 in `apps/server/src/realtime/gateway.integration.test.ts`, through the harness's `apps/server/src/testing/valkey-expiry.ts`;
- **Guards:** `scripts/guards/design-ids.test.ts` (LCG-01 and LCG-07), `scripts/guards/lifecycle.test.ts` (LCG-02 to LCG-05), `scripts/guards/later-steps.test.ts` (LCG-06) and `scripts/guards/runbooks.test.ts` (RB-06).

The classes, their figures and the vocabularies live in `packages/shared/src/retention.ts` (`RETENTION_CLASSES`, `REFUSED_COPY_HOLD`, `HOLD_KINDS`, `HOLD_TARGET_KINDS`, `DELETION_CAUSES`, `CLOUDWATCH_RETENTION_DAYS`, and the refused-copy mirror `heldUntil()`), which migration 0013 mirrors (`retention_class_defaults()`, `refused_copy_hold()`, `lifecycle_kinds()`, the `legal_holds_target_kind` check and `deletion_causes()`), 0014 replacing `retention_class_defaults()` and `lifecycle_kinds()` with the same signatures for the provenance tables and `derived`, and CI holds the two equal both ways (check 7). The delete paths are decided in `apps/server/src/lifecycle/deletes.ts` (`DELETE_PATHS` and `GUARDED_TABLES`; check 8). C0.10 adds no server variable, route, screen, string, e2e spec, job kind, schedule, role, consumer or event (item 1).

1. **Scope and split (LH-01, LH-29).** C0.10 is built in two PRs, each with its own migration and owner read: the risks live now are PR 1's, since the provenance registry has no writer, reader or grant until C1.2, C3.5 and A2.3, and the owner reads two smaller migrations (one PR and one migration, or four PRs like C0.9's, were the alternatives).
    - **PR 1, migration 0013 (holds and the deletion code):** the retention-class registry and `retention_period_days()` (items 2 to 6); the deletion log (item 7); the guard on `cases`, `case_steps` and `legal_holds` (item 8); every delete path decided (item 9); the `legal_holds` table and its place in the hold decision (item 10); access-request holds (item 11); `is_held()` and the containment seam (item 12); `delete_case()` and an index on `processing_restrictions (org_id, case_id)` (item 13); the clean-up's exemption recorded (item 15); OD-27's revoke (item 16); checks 7 and 8, the composite-key check, the DB tests, the races, its upgrade check, and the unit, integration and guard cases (item 19); and the docs: this section, the BUILD-PLAN rows that inherit, PRD B.6's lifecycle row, CLAUDE.md, and the runbooks `access-request.md`, `withdrawal-by-hand.md` and `README.md`. C0.10's Done is met in two halves: "held items refuse deletion" by PR 1 (LCD-27 to LCD-30, LCR-01 to LCR-04), and "deleting a source removes its derived rows" by PR 2 (LCD-36). C0a's exit waits for both.
    - **PR 2, migration 0014 (provenance):** item 14, with five of PR 1's functions replaced with the same signatures (`retention_class_defaults()`, moving the two tables into `provenance`, and `lifecycle_kinds()`, `lifecycle_persons_of()`, `lifecycle_row_anchors()` and `lifecycle_sources_of()`), `derivedFacts()` in `packages/access`, `scripts/check-access-sql.ts` over the stored registry, and R3-01b and R7-11 running.
    - **No route, screen, string or e2e spec (LH-29).** C0.10's row names no PRD row, and every requirement it serves (ADM-07, ADM-08, PDPA-09) has its screens in C1.11 or C2.9 (D18). The bundle, the visual baselines, `GET /api/session`, the DPO's pages, Settings › Privacy and the notice are unchanged, and the case sheet shows no hold.
    - **The number.** The sole Owner's break-glass PIN reset (C0.9 OD-28) had also planned 0013. C0.10's PR 1 took it, so the reset's PR takes the next free number, "with its own migration" (T14 item 21, T22 items 63 and 71, BUILD-PLAN C0.5); `scripts/check-migration-order.sh` refuses a number at or below the base's highest.

    It leaves out, each in its BUILD-PLAN row (item 20): the log groups', traces', RUM's and CloudTrail's retention from the registry, the log-archive bucket and the break-glass runbook's replica-mode rule (C0.11, C0.13); buckets per S3 class with Object Lock at creation, and ElastiCache's snapshot and slow-log settings (C0.12); the stream trim and the "Legal holds changed" alert (C0.14); the first S3 deletions, quarantine copies included (C0.15); the audit viewer's hold filter and the hash chain's deletion boundary (C0.16); the deletion log's shipper, its copy outside the database and replay (C0.17); holds on conversations and messages (C1.1); `isHeld()` in edit and delete, held versions and cited provenance (C1.2); the hold writers, their read, access actions and audit filter (C1.11); holds on rooms and recorded sessions (C2.4); a session page's "kept until", the policy date and never a hold (C2.8); every retention sweep, the guard on each table a sweep deletes from, the per-org settings and a leaver's reduction (C2.9); a restriction citing a case, and corrections that withdraw (C2.11); producers of provenance (C3.5, C4.3, C4.4, A2.3); Drive holds and the Trash's purge (C4.1); the DPO's deletion-log read, narrowing an access-request hold to what the request covers, held versions and the PDPA's exception for an investigation not yet complete, and the legal-hold export (C6.1); who sets the AI classes (A0.1); sources and feedback from provenance (A0.4); and people inferences (A3.3, A3.4).

2. **The registry (LH-03, LH-05, LH-07, LH-57, LH-58).** Classes are code, `RETENTION_CLASSES` in `packages/shared/src/retention.ts`, which `infra/` can import at synth time. `retention_class_defaults()` mirrors the 43 classes whose data Postgres holds or whose objects S3 holds, with the facts the deletion code reads (`key`, `store`, `ai`, `period_kind` (`days`, `follows`, `while` or `until`), `days`, `anchor`, `follows`, `holds` (`apply`, `exempt` or `kept`), `tables` and `objects` (whether the class has S3 objects: every `s3` class, and `drive_items` and `drive_trash`, whose rows go with their files; R2DB-01)); Valkey's, the logs', the backups' and pub/sub's classes are code's and `infra/`'s alone (item 6).
    - **No per-org rows (LH-03).** Nothing in C0 changes a period per org, the first setter being C2.9's (ADM-07), and a trigger on `orgs` would add rows to the Owner bootstrap's pinned write set (BSS-03, T22 item 66) for no reader. C2.9 adds the per-org settings table, its setter and the backfill with its first tunable class, and replaces `retention_period_days()` with the same signature (item 5). A step that adds a class or moves a table into one replaces `retention_class_defaults()` with the same signature, and a class key is never removed, since `deletion_log` rows name it.
    - **Fields (LH-05):** store, tables, what it covers, purposes, `ai`, stage, period (with its range in code), status, who sets it, whether it may be indefinite (a class kept while the org exists carries its written justification), holds (`apply`, `exempt` with the decision that records why, or `kept`), what deletes it, the function bodies a fixed age lives in (`fixedIn`), and S3 objects with Object Lock. Statuses: `decided` (D36's figures, FILE-02's Trash), `proposed` (PDPA-09's and Q13's proposals, and LH-53's), `fixed` (an age in a migration) and `dpo` (as set by the DPO). Ranges live in code only, as CLAUDE.md asks of every setting's range; no setter reads them before C2.9, whose migration may add checks.
    - **The 54 classes, in four groups** (C0.10's 52, and from C0.11 `break_glass_log` and `network_logs`, TECH-SPEC T24 item 9). C0's data, every table of `public` in exactly one (item 3): `org_settings` (`orgs`, `audit_log_readers`, kept while the org exists), `roster` (its eight tables, kept while the org exists, a leaver's profile reduced by C2.9), `working_hours` (replaced by the person's own edit, which is exempt: LH-48), `sign_in_records` (`phone_numbers`, `devices`, `sessions` and `passkeys`, 731 days after the record ended, admins from C2.9 within 365 to 2,190 days), `acknowledgements` (`notice_acknowledgments`, while the person's other data is kept), `audit_log` (731 days from `at`, never below 365: ADM-06), `access_log` (`data_access_events`, 731 days, a month's partition dropped only once it holds no row), `outbox`, `jobs` and `schedule_runs` (the clean-up's fixed ages, item 15), `service_config` (flags, schedules and the outbox's positions, with no personal data), `compliance_records` (the registers, the notice's tables and `dpo_settings`, kept while the org exists), `cases` (item 5), `legal_holds` (731 days after release), `deletion_log` (731 days, item 7) and `provenance` (following its item: `provenance_items` and `provenance_sources`, from 0014). Later content, each step moving its tables in: `profile_photos` and `upload_quarantine` (C0.15), `messages` (C1.1, 1,095 days, Q13's proposal), `held_versions` (C1.2, kept only while a hold needs them), `voice_message_audio` (C1.6, 360 days, D36), `derived_caches` (from C1.7, following their source), `breach_register` (C1.11, as the DPO sets, 731 days after closure by default), `tasks` (C2.1), `session_records` (C2.4, 1,095 days), `session_media` (C2.6, 120 days, D36, with Object Lock), `calendar_events` (C3.1), `drive_items` and `drive_trash` (C4.1, Postgres classes with S3 objects; the Trash 30 days, FILE-02), `slide_copies` (C5.6, following the session's record until A1) and `people_insights` (C6.10). The AI classes (item 4). The stores outside Postgres (item 6).
    - **LH-53's figures,** which the PRD doesn't give: app logs 90 days, RUM 30 days, CloudTrail 731 days; AI cost records 731 days; the transcription test set 731 days after consent, and at once on withdrawal; personal memory 365 days after its last update, and at deactivation; AI feedback 365 days after it was given, and with the output it rates; people inferences 365 days after they were made; session records 1,095 days; the breach register 731 days after closure; released legal holds 731 days after release; the deletion log 731 days; quarantine copies a day at most. Each is a declaration until its step arrives.
    - **Who changes what.** The clean-up's and the log's ages: nobody but a migration (check 7c pins them). Valkey's figures: nobody but a reviewed code change, since they are security controls. `audit_log`, `access_log`, `sign_in_records`, the content classes and D36's audio classes: Owners and Admins from C2.9, within the range in code, the DPO alerted (C0.14). `cases` and `breach_register`: the DPO alone from C2.9 (item 5). The AI classes: nobody until A0.1 decides (item 4). Logs and backups: `infra/`, reading the registry at synth time (C0.11, C0.13, C0.17). The classes kept while the org exists: nobody, with their written justification in code and PDPA-09's annual review reminder from C2.9, with C0.14's categories.
    - **C2.9's rule for changing a period (LH-38).** A period applies to history from the stored anchor at use (CLAUDE.md, Database), so a shortening deletes a backlog at the next sweep. C2.9's setter shows, before it saves, how many rows of each class become due; makes a shortening take effect only after a fixed notice period in code, the old period applying meanwhile; alerts the DPO, with ids and counts only; and applies a lengthening only once the published notice states it, the shorter stated period applying until then, as a request's due day takes the shorter of the stated and current targets (C0.9 PD-92). Leaving the rule to C2.9 was the alternative; it binds the registry's design now.
    - **Quarantine copies (LH-57).** C0.15's upload copies in quarantine are the class `upload_quarantine`: deleted, every version, when promoted, re-encoded or refused by the scan, and at most a day after upload meanwhile; exempt from holds, since the promoted or re-encoded object is what a hold keeps (confirmed under LH-48); but logged, with cause `upload` and a `quarantine/` key, so C0.15 changes no C0.10 table.
    - **A leaver's reduction (LH-58).** When C2.9 reduces a leaver's profile to name and title, clears their PIN hash and deletes their working hours, it is deletion code: a held-checked definer that takes the person `FOR NO KEY UPDATE`, asks `lifecycle_held()`, skips a held person, audits each reduction and logs each working-hours row it deletes with `log_deletion()`, a `checked` delete path (item 9). The person's own working-hours edit stays exempt, since refusing a held person's edit would tell them of the hold.
    - **Merged migrations that name C0.10 for sweeps.** 0003's comments promise clearing expired and revoked sessions and deleting devices, and 0005's dropping `data_access_events` partitions, to "the retention engine (C0.10)". Those sweeps are C2.9's (BUILD-PLAN C0: "no retention sweeps (registry, holds and deletion log only)"). A merged migration never changes, so this item restates who does what, as T15 item 16 restated 0005's "monthly".
    - **The purposes deletion serves.** Deletion is a privacy control, so nothing may cancel it: no flag gates one, and every deletion job and cascade consumer (C2.9's sweeps, C0.17's replay, C0.15's S3 retry) runs under `core.system` with purpose `service_operation`, which is pinned and passes the purpose gate in every state. A deletion-log row falls under `service_operation` through its class, so the DPO's free-text retention for `service_operation` covers the log's 731 days; a tombstone carries the deleted item's own purpose; an access-request hold needs no purpose key; and the `legal_holds` purpose key arrives with C1.11's first writer (LH-36, item 10). `compliance_records` names `accounts` for now (item 20).

3. **Every table in a class; fixed ages pinned (LH-04).** Every relation of `public` that isn't a partition (the migrator's ledger aside), the 35 that 0001 to 0012 create, 0013's two and 0014's two, is in exactly one class's `tables`, and every name a class lists is such a table (check 7b, LCS-01, LCD-02), so a new table without a retention decision fails its own PR. `data_access_events`' month partitions belong to their parent's class. Where an age is fixed in SQL, the class's `fixedIn` names each function and its intervals, and check 7c holds them equal to the `interval '<n> days'` literals in that function's body, read with comments stripped and never through the reader that empties string literals: `purge_outbox` 14 days, `purge_jobs` 14 and 30 days, `purge_schedule_runs` 90 days, `deletion_log_kept` 731 days. The alternatives were classes only for what later steps need (a table could reach production with no decision) and trusting comments (the clean-up's ages could drift from the registry).

4. **The AI classes (LH-06).** Declared now with finite defaults and nothing more (BUILD-PLAN C0.10, D41): `transcripts` (1,095 days after the session ended), `voice_message_transcripts` (1,095 days after sent), `machine_output` (following `transcripts`), `ai_notes` (1,095 days), `brain_index` (following its source: Rule 7's withdrawal), `ai_conversations` (365 days after the last message, PDPA-15), `agent_memory` (365 days after last updated), `personal_memory` (at deactivation, and 365 days after last updated meanwhile), `ai_cost_records` (731 days), `transcription_test_set` (731 days after consent, at once on withdrawal: §9.5), `ai_feedback` (365 days after it was given, and with the output it rates: AMB-06's thumbs up and down) and `people_inferences` (365 days after made, withdrawn with its sources: SRCH-05, BRAIN-05). Every one is `ai`, finite and never indefinite (D12), holds apply, and it names AI purposes only; every purpose with `ai` in `PURPOSES` is in at least one (LCS-03, LCS-03b, check 7e). None has a table, a per-org value or a setter, so nothing changes them before A0.1 decides who sets them (ADM-05, ADM-07's AI part): the same lock an AI purpose has. If C2.9's settings list classes, they leave `ai` classes out as `NON_AI_PURPOSE_KEYS` does, and hold their copy to `AI_COPY`. A1 to A5 move their tables in with no migration for the class (declaring them in A1 to A5 instead was the alternative).

5. **The cases class, and its period in SQL (LH-08, LH-09).** `cases` covers `cases` and `case_steps`, requesters' names, channels and addresses included, under `request_handling`: 731 days after `closed_on`, for every outcome (LH-47); status `dpo` (PDPA-09: "as set by the DPO") with a proposed default; range 90 to 3,650 days; never indefinite; an open request is never due.
    - **`retention_period_days(org, class)`** gives a `days` class's period in whole days in SQL (731 for `cases`), raising 22023 for an unknown key or a class with no day count. It takes the org already, so its signature never changes when C2.9 replaces it to read its settings table first and every caller moves with it.
    - **Due-ness in SQL.** `delete_case()` applies the period itself, so the age is in SQL from the first delete path, not only in C2.9's sweep (T15 item 17: "so no worker bug can delete early"); leaving due-ness to the sweep was the alternative.
    - **From C2.9** the DPO's period becomes a structured value, a whole number of days within the range, beside `request_handling`'s free-text retention in its processing-register entry, through the existing `register.purpose.set`; each change is a new register version, which makes the notice out of date, and the period-change rule applies (LH-38). The DPO keeps the register's text and the class's figure consistent until C2.9 prints the number in fixed words. Rejected: a `dpo_settings` column (every version copies every column and publishing compares the version, so an unprinted column would make every notice out of date for nothing, and a printed one would state the period twice); no default until the DPO sets one (PDPA-09 says finite by default); a setter now (nothing reads it before C2.9).

6. **Stores outside Postgres (LH-07).** Exempt classes, each with its longest life pinned to the code or service that sets it, and outside holds by name (LH-48, whose list C0.11's two logs classes extend with the DPO's confirmation: TECH-SPEC T24 item 9, C11-Q10). Valkey's expiry is a security control, not a delete path `isHeld()` could stop, and its facts are in the audit rows a hold keeps; leaving these out of the registry was the alternative, but PDPA-09 asks for a period per data type, and C0.11 and C0.13 read log retention from the registry.
    - **Valkey.** `valkey_sign_in`: `attempt` and `otp` 600,000 ms, `challenge` and `reauthApproved` 120,000 ms, `reauthSending` 15,000 ms (`apps/server/src/auth/policy.ts`). `valkey_limits`: `limit`, each window's key kept for its own window (`apps/server/src/auth/limiter.ts`), so a day at most, the longest window in `LIMITS`; the design's "under twice the longest window, 172,800,000 ms" was wrong, since the limiter expires each key after its own window. `valkey_realtime`: `stream` and `epoch`, 86,400,000 ms after the last refresh (`GATEWAY_POLICY.streamTtlMs`, `apps/server/src/realtime/policy.ts`), the stream holding no entry until C0.14, which trims it on refresh before its first producer. `valkey_channels`: `revoked`, `access` and `flags`, pub/sub, nothing stored. Keyed hashes and ids only, never restored. LCV-01 holds every figure equal to its `policy.ts` expression, iterating `LIMITS`, so a new window fails until it has a figure; LCV-02 holds the families equal to `valkeyKeys()`'s functions and channels both ways; LCI-01 and LCI-02 scan the run's key prefix after the sign-in and realtime suites and require every key's remaining life above 0 and at most its family's figure, so a key written without expiry fails CI.
    - **Logs.** `app_logs` 90 days (every role's CloudWatch log groups, the migrate step's included, and from C0.11 the readiness probe's and the bastion's), `traces` 30 days (X-Ray, fixed by AWS), `rum` 30 days (C0.13), `cloudtrail` 731 days (the organisation trail in the log-archive account, with Object Lock, and from C0.11 its CloudWatch Logs copy in the management account for the alarms and the quarterly review), and C0.11's two (C11-D22, TECH-SPEC T24 item 9): `break_glass_log` 731 days (each break-glass use's open and close entries, references and ids only, in the log-archive account's bucket with Object Lock, as long as the CloudTrail events they explain) and `network_logs` 90 days (every VPC's flow logs, client and internal addresses), each in days CloudWatch accepts (`CLOUDWATCH_RETENTION_DAYS`, LCS-07), set by `infra/` from the registry at synth time. Being logs classes, the two need no SQL mirror and no migration. A held person's log lines aren't exported before they expire: the audit log keeps the facts, and holds cover it.
    - **Backups.** 35 days (RDS point-in-time recovery and AWS Backup, C0.17). A backup ageing out leaves the live copy, which the deletion code protects, and a restore replays the deletion log (item 7).
    - **ElastiCache.** C0.12 sets its snapshot retention to 0, with no persistence and slow-log delivery off, or the registry gains each with its period.

7. **The deletion log (LH-10, LH-51).**
    - **A row** per deleted entity (one row of its table, with the rows that belong only to it and go with it: a request's steps, from 0014 a provenance item's sources), written by `log_deletion()` in the deleting transaction, so the row and the delete commit together or not at all. It holds the org; the transaction's id (`tx_id`, the column's default); when; the class; the entity's table and id (a lower-case uuid, or a whole number for `audit_events` alone, whose ids are bigint: `deletion_log_object`, so no number, name or reference stands in for an id; PRIVACY-01); the cause; for a deletion on a request, the case's id (a plain id that outlives the case); for a cascade, the parent row's id; `replayed`; and for an S3 object (from C0.15) its data class and key, `<org_id>/<random id>` or `quarantine/<org_id>/<random id>`, never another org's or a filename, and only with a class with S3 objects (every `s3` class, and `drive_items` and `drive_trash`, whose row and its file are logged as one row carrying the key, so `deletion_log_entity` never meets a second; R2DB-01), which `log_deletion()` checks (PRIVACY-01), with `s3_done_at` once every version and delete marker is gone. Nothing else: no actor, subject, name, filename, title, reference or reason. Who caused a deletion is in its audit row, one per action, or one per sweep batch from C2.9; a sweep that deletes 5,000 audit rows writes 5,000 log rows. One row per entity per transaction (`deletion_log_entity`).
    - **Causes in a function.** `deletion_causes()` lists `retention`, `request`, `cascade`, `redaction`, `user`, `trash`, `offboarding`, `correction` and `upload`, which `log_deletion()` checks, with the class (one whose `tables` holds the entity's table, or an `s3` class for an object; an S3 class or key only with a class with S3 objects, PRIVACY-01, R2DB-01; 22023, "That deletion can't be logged."). The column's check holds only the shape, so a new cause is a function replacement, never a change to C0.10's largest table under ACCESS EXCLUSIVE (a cause CHECK was the alternative). A replay is no cause: it re-logs the original cause and case with `replayed` set, so a second restore can still tell a request's deletion from a retention one. `DELETION_CAUSES` equals the function (check 7f; LCS-08: no `replay`).
    - **Append-only, for every writer.** `deletion_log_kept()`, the owner's and a superuser's writes included, allows one change, an S3 deletion's stamp, set once on a row with a key (CLAUDE.md's "stamps the log row"), and no delete of a row younger than 731 days: the class's fixed age, at least the 35-day backup window plus a restore and as long as the audit log by default, which check 7c holds to the body. It fires on INSERT too: a new row's `at` and `tx_id` must be the database's (`now()` and `pg_current_xact_id()`, the columns' defaults, which `log_deletion()` never sets), so no writer can backdate a row to delete a guarded row and then its log row, or log for another transaction (GUARD-01; 23514). `lifecycle_no_truncate()` refuses TRUNCATE; nothing references the log, so a plain TRUNCATE reaches the trigger. C2.9 adds the purge of older rows, one audit row per batch, with its check-8 entry.
    - **No grant, reader or event.** RLS with the one org policy would bind any future grantee. C2.9 adds counts under item 18's counts rule, C0.17's replay reads it as the restore's operator, and C6.1's console gives the DPO "was this deleted, and when" by object id, never by person; a person's access request finds nothing personal in it, by design. No event: the log is itself the record (coverage's `DELETION_LOG`), and the outbox is purged in 14 days and rolled back by a restore too.
    - **The restore contract (LH-51),** which C0.11, C0.12 and C0.17 build with no change to a C0.10 table:
      - **The copy.** A shipper with its own job role and checkpoint reads `deletion_log` only, in `(tx_id, id)` order below the oldest running transaction, as the outbox's consumers do (T16 item 2), so no row commits behind it, and writes its rows, ids only, to the log-archive account's bucket with Object Lock (C0.11, C0.12). Its lag bounds what a restore can bring back (an RPO of 15 minutes, C0.17).
      - **Its restore check.** At start it runs T16 item 18's future-rows check against `deletion_log` and its own checkpoint: a stored `tx_id` at or above `pg_snapshot_xmax(pg_current_snapshot())` stops it and leaves the worker unready, since after a logical restore or a Blue/Green switchover new rows would sort behind its checkpoint and never ship. The Blue/Green and logical-restore runbooks move its checkpoint to the new cluster's position with the consumers', once the archive holds every row copied before the switch.
      - **Hold and case facts.** Each run it also writes the current hold facts (each hold's id, target kind and id, case id, placed and released times and principals, never a reason) and each case's hold facts (its id, requester id, `closed_kind`, `outcome`, `closed_on`, and each review step's kind and day): ids, codes and dates only.
      - **Replay** runs before any worker, consumer or api serves the restored copy. For every row in the union of the restored log, the live log if it survives and the archive, it calls that entity's deletion function with the original cause and case and the replay flag set: a row already gone is a no-op (`delete_case()` answers `gone`); a row present is deleted, logged again with `replayed` and tombstoned where its kind has a tombstone. A cascade's rows are re-made by replaying their source. The outbox's, the queue's and the runs' purges are outside it, since their restored rows are cleaned again within a day.
      - **Holds and periods on replay.** Replay checks holds for every cause, with no bypass in the guard or in `delete_case()`, and the period only where the original cause was `retention`: an early deletion (a request, a person's own delete, a redaction, the Trash, offboarding, a cascade, a correction, an upload copy) was never due under its class's period and is replayed regardless of it, so deleted data doesn't reappear. Anything kept (held now, or a retention deletion not due under a period lengthened since) stays, which fails safe, and goes on replay's own retry list (ids only, in the archive), tried daily until done; the operator sees one combined kept count, never what or why. A bypass that deletes regardless, which any role able to set it could misuse, was the alternative.
      - **Before anything but replay deletes.** The restored copy carries a restore marker (C0.17), and every other delete path (C2.9's sweeps, a person's delete, the Trash) stays paused until two confirmations are recorded: the DPO's, that requests, closures and PDPC review steps recorded after the restore point are re-entered through the DPO's own pages with their real days; and a hold reader's, that holds placed after it are re-placed through `place_legal_hold()` with their own reasons. Each works from the archived facts the runbook hands them. Releases made after the restore point aren't re-applied by hand: a hold reader releases again, with a stated reason. Nobody writes a hold or a case row as the owner.

8. **The guard (LH-11, LH-12, LH-13).**
    - **What it checks.** `lifecycle_guard('<table>')`, a BEFORE DELETE row trigger, refuses any role's DELETE of a row, the owner's and a superuser's included, unless the row's deletion is logged in the same transaction (a `deletion_log` row for its entity with this transaction's `tx_id`; HF010 otherwise) and nothing holds it (`lifecycle_held()`; HF009 otherwise). `lifecycle_no_truncate()`, a BEFORE TRUNCATE statement trigger, always refuses (HF010). The guard reads the org from the row, so a delete with no `app.org_id` (the owner by hand) is still checked. It is SECURITY DEFINER with `search_path = ''`, so it reads `deletion_log`, `cases` and `legal_holds` whichever role deletes; it takes no acting user and writes nothing. Its messages are fixed and name no hold. Checking only in the deletion functions was the alternative, which a future grant or a careless definer would skip.
    - **Its table named by argument,** never `tg_table_name`: a row trigger on a partitioned table is cloned onto each partition and fires with the partition's name, while the clone keeps the argument, so C2.9's first sweep of `data_access_events`' partitions logs and anchors as the parent with no new guard code (LCD-09 proves it on a scratch partition).
    - **Where it sits, and why only there (LH-12).** On `cases`, `case_steps` and `legal_holds` in PR 1, and on `provenance_items` and `provenance_sources` from PR 2 (0014); `deletion_log` carries `lifecycle_no_truncate()` beside `deletion_log_kept()`. `GUARDED_TABLES` lists exactly these, and check 7d holds the triggers to it. The other holds-apply tables (`phone_numbers`, `devices`, `sessions`, `passkeys`, `notice_acknowledgments`, `audit_events` and `data_access_events`) have no delete path in C0: the only runtime DELETE grant is `hearth_app`'s on `working_hours`, and no function deletes from them: the clean-up's three delete only from the outbox, the queue and its runs, and the deletion code (`delete_case()`, and from 0014 `lifecycle_cascade()` and `provenance_forget()`) only from guarded tables (item 9). A trigger on each would lock the busiest tables under the migration's window for no deletion, and change two DB tests that delete a device as the superuser to prove `ON DELETE SET NULL`. Each gets the guard with its first delete path, which check 8 forces (item 9), and its anchors exist now (check 7g calls them), so the guard arrives with no new code. C2.9 measures the per-row cost on `audit_events` and `data_access_events`, and may decide a batch in one query with a statement-level trigger over a transition table instead.
    - **Anchors.** `lifecycle_row_anchors(org, table, row)` gives the guard a row's hold kind, item id and people, for every holds-apply table, guarded or not: a number's person; a device's, sign-in session's or passkey's account; an acknowledgement's person; a case's requester; a request's step, which anchors as its request (the guard decides a step only by its request's log row, once the request has gone); a legal hold's target, when it is a person; an access-log row's subject, actor and the person acted for; and an audit row's actor, the person acted for, a target person, the DPO who decided a clearance (`details.decided_by`, which `bootstrap_transfer_clearance()` writes with Hearth as the actor), the people a flag's cohort change added or removed (`details.people_added` and `details.people_removed`, which `set_feature_flag()` writes, 0007; R2DB-02), and the requester of a case it names by `target_id` or `case_id` (its item null, since its id is a whole number). From 0014 a provenance item anchors as its item (`item_kind`, `item_id`), with its own kind's people when `lifecycle_kinds()` knows the kind (none for `derived`, which is about nobody itself, since `lifecycle_held()` takes its people from its live sources; a later step's suggested task, its owner), and none for a kind Hearth can't judge yet, which the cascade and the leftover sweep keep before they delete; and a provenance source anchors as its item, by which the guard decides a source once its item has gone. Any other table raises 22023.
    - **Children after parents (LH-13).** `case_steps`' key to `cases` is now `deferrable initially deferred`, altered under 0013's lock window. `delete_case()` deletes the request, whose guard decides with every step present, then the steps, whose guard requires the request gone and logged ("A step is deleted only with its request, after it.", HF010), and the key is checked at the commit. Children first would let a step's deletion (`review_started`, say) end the request's hold before the request's own guard decided. Every insert path locks its request first (`add_case_step()`), so a deferred check changes no insert's answer. Since the key is deferred, a TRUNCATE in a transaction that already wrote `case_steps` is refused with 55006 (pending trigger events) before the guard runs: still refused. A plain TRUNCATE of `cases`, which other tables reference, fails earlier on Postgres's own foreign-key check (0A000), and `TRUNCATE … CASCADE` reaches the triggers (LCD-10).
    - **Enabled the default way (`O`), never ALWAYS.** ALWAYS would also fire in C0.11's logical replication (Blue/Green, T16 item 18), where the apply worker's transaction ids differ from the copied log rows' and `app.org_id` is unset, so every replicated delete would fail; the subscriber receives only deletions the publisher already checked. Check 7d refuses a guard that is missing, not enabled `O`, named for the wrong table, or given a WHEN condition or an UPDATE OF list, on a parent table or a partition's clone (GUARD-04). A trigger enabled `O` doesn't fire under `session_replication_role = replica`, which nothing sets in production (C0.11's break-glass runbook never does), and LCG-05 keeps the repository's replica-mode code from deleting guarded rows.
    - **What it can't stop (DB-07).** `hearth_owner` owns the guarded tables and the trigger functions, so anyone holding the owner's credential (the migrate login, the bootstrap login, break-glass) can disable, drop or replace the guard by DDL, with no superuser, and then delete; a superuser can also set replica mode. Each is a direct production change CLAUDE.md forbids. Check 7d catches it in any migration on a fresh build, LCG-03 refuses it in later migrations unless reviewed, LCG-05 covers replica mode in code, and C0.11 logs production access and weighs an `infra/` event trigger, created by the RDS master user, refusing that DDL on the guarded tables outside the migrate login. A dropped or detached partition, a TRUNCATE of one partition, a dropped table and S3 aren't seen by triggers either: check 8 (its `DYNAMIC_DDL` rule for format strings; `bodyDdlProblems` for a body's plain statements, a `DYNAMIC_DDL` function's strings and its pinned body), LCG-02 and LCG-03 cover them.
    - **Cost.** One indexed lookup and one hold decision per deleted row, nothing on an insert or update. The decision locks the row's people `FOR NO KEY UPDATE`, so a sign-in of one of them waits for the deleting transaction (bounded by its 30 s, normally milliseconds). One `flag.set` audit row can anchor up to 1,000 people added and 1,000 removed (its cohort's limit, T17 item 3), so its hold decision locks that many people rows; C2.9 measures that for its audit sweep (R2DB-02).

9. **Delete paths (LH-14).** `DELETE_PATHS` in `apps/server/src/lifecycle/deletes.ts` decides every delete path the database allows, as `coverage.ts` decides writes, and check 8 finds them as check 1 finds writes: every DELETE grant of a runtime role (`'<role> <table>:DELETE'`); every function of `public` whose body deletes, a plain helper no runtime role may run included, since a definer can call it (GUARD-03); every foreign key with an ON DELETE action (`'fk <table>.<constraint>'`); and every `DYNAMIC_DDL` entry (`'ddl <function>(<args>)'`). Each entry is `guarded` (its tables carry the guard, and it says what it is to the provenance registry: `forgets`, its rows can be a source or an item and its body calls `provenance_forget()`; `notASource`, its rows never are, with the decision or requirement id that records why; or `registry`, the registry's own deletion code, `lifecycle_cascade()` and `provenance_forget()` alone, by key, deleting from its two tables alone, which no other path deletes from; item 14), `checked` (a definer that asks `lifecycle_held()` or `is_held()` itself, skips a held row and logs each row it deletes with `log_deletion()`, only for a table whose class marks a leaver's reduction (`reduction: 'apply'`), rows a person also edits, where a trigger would reveal a hold: today `working_hours` alone; LH-58, GUARD-02) or `exempt`, with the decision that records why. Review alone was the alternative, which a new grant or a partition drop would slip past. Check 8 also refuses, in any function body of `public`, TRUNCATE, DROP TABLE, DETACH PARTITION, a trigger disabled, dropped or enabled ALWAYS or REPLICA, a setting of `session_replication_role`, a replacement, drop or change of the guard's functions or `lifecycle_held()`, a guard's trigger replaced (CREATE OR REPLACE TRIGGER naming a guard trigger or function), and a guard trigger created with a WHEN condition or an UPDATE OF list, each as a plain statement and in a `DYNAMIC_DDL` function's string literals (`bodyDdlProblems`). None of these is a DELETE a path could decide (GUARD-05). Each `DYNAMIC_DDL` entry also pins its function's body (comments and whitespace aside), so a migration that replaces it fails check 8 until the new body is pinned in `catalogues.ts`, a reviewed change: a verb passed to `format()` as an argument, or a string built at run time, is read by no other check (R2CODE-01).
    - **In C0:** `purge_outbox()`, `purge_jobs()` and `purge_schedule_runs()`, and the two keys only the last fires (`jobs.schedule_run_id` and `schedule_runs.resumed_from`, SET NULL), exempt under LH-26 (item 15); `hearth_app`'s `working_hours` DELETE, exempt under LH-48 (a person's own settings, replaced by their own edit, `profile.updated` recording each change); the keys that drop a deleted device's link from an acknowledgement and a passkey, exempt under LH-48 (the record outlives the device); `create_data_access_partitions()`'s dynamic DDL, which creates partitions and deletes nothing (T15 item 16); `delete_case()`, guarded and `notASource` under RQS-11 (a request is never indexed, derived from or cited, which both provenance tables' kind checks refuse too); and from 0014 `lifecycle_cascade()` and `provenance_forget()`, guarded and `registry` (each body deletes items and their edges from the two provenance tables, both in `GUARDED_TABLES`). No CASCADE key exists: the edges' key to their item has no ON DELETE action, and is deferred so an item goes before its edges.
    - **What a path deletes from (DB-10).** A grant or a function: the tables its DELETEs name. A CASCADE key: its child. A SET NULL or SET DEFAULT key: nothing, since it is an UPDATE of its child, and the deletion that fires it is decided under its parent's own entry.
    - **It fails on** a path with no entry, or an entry naming nothing; a `guarded` entry that deletes from a table not in `GUARDED_TABLES`, so a step's first delete path brings the guard; a `guarded` entry that doesn't say what it is to the provenance registry; a `forgets` entry whose body (comments and strings stripped) never calls `provenance_forget(`, or that is a grant or a key, which can call nothing; a `notASource` entry whose decision is neither one check 8 reads nor a requirement or test id this section cites (`recordedReasons()`); any path but `lifecycle_cascade()` and `provenance_forget()` that deletes from `provenance_items` or `provenance_sources`, whatever it says, a grant or a key included, since one that deleted a record itself would skip the cascade to what was made from it and leave the edges naming it live; a `registry` entry for any other path, or either of the two saying anything but `registry`; a `registry` entry that deletes from a table other than the two; so every item's delete path forgets what it deletes (item 14); a `checked` entry whose body never calls `lifecycle_held(` or `is_held(`, or never calls `log_deletion(`, or that deletes from a table whose class marks no leaver's reduction or from a guarded or kept one, so every other holds-apply table's first path is `guarded` and brings its guard (GUARD-02); an `exempt` entry that deletes from a holds-apply table; a decision that isn't an LH id this section records or a TECH-SPEC "T<n> item <m>" whose section holds that item (check 8 reads both from `docs/TECH-SPEC.md`); a CASCADE key into a guarded table whose child isn't guarded or listed; a `DYNAMIC_DDL` body that names `drop`, `truncate` (but in a trigger's event list), `detach` or `delete`; a `DYNAMIC_DDL` function whose body isn't the one pinned with it; a function check 1 doesn't reach whose body check 8 can't read (BEGIN ATOMIC, or EXECUTE outside `DYNAMIC_DDL`; `helperBodyProblems`, GUARD-03); and any delete path to a kept table. So `DYNAMIC_DDL`'s documented meaning, "only changes the schema and writes no row", is checked rather than trusted.
    - **Later:** C1.2's message delete and edit, C4.1's purge and the Trash's clean-up, C2.9's sweeps, its leaver reduction (`checked`, LH-58) and `deletion_log`'s purge, and C0.15's S3 deletions each add their entries with their first delete path, and a `guarded` path's tables to `GUARDED_TABLES`.
